#7193. Senior ML Engineer - Malware Detection (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

Imunify360 is CloudLinux's security suite for Linux web servers: real-time and background malware scanning, automated cleanup, a WAF, runtime PHP protection (Proactive Defense), a database malware scanner, and IP reputation. It runs on hosting providers' shared, VPS, and dedicated servers, so new web-malware campaigns land on our fleet early.

We sit on one of the largest labeled corpora of live web-server malware in the industry: files, verdicts, cleanup outcomes, and analyst decisions collected from production hosting servers every day. It already drives our signatures, heuristics, and ML models, and there is far more to get out of it. We are looking for a Senior ML Engineer who will turn that data into detection that ships — classifiers, LLM-assisted triage and generated signatures — and keep exploring new ML/LLM methods against it, measured by the product's false-negative and false-positive rates, not by notebook accuracy.

Our dataset focuses on web malware — PHP, JavaScript, HTML phishing kits, infected WordPress and Joomla plugins and themes, database injections — with the context around each file.

The goal is 10x more malware detected at the same or lower false-positive rate. Hand-written rules and faster pipelines will not get there; models trained on our data might.

You get an experimentation track, GPUs, and a direct line to leadership; in return, you bring numbers, not opinions.

You own the path from raw samples to a model in production, and the numbers that prove it helped.

#7190. Senior ML Engineer - Email Security (remote work) at CloudLinux

CloudLinux is a global remote-first company. We are driven by our principles: do the right thing, employees first, we are remote first, and we deliver high-volume, low-cost Linux infrastructure and security products that help companies to increase the efficiency of their operations. Every person on our team supports each other and does what we can to ensure we all are successful.

Check out our website for more information: https://cloudlinux.com/

CloudLinux Inc. develops the Imunify family of security products, designed to protect hosting environments from a wide range of cyber threats. One of these solutions is Imunify Email, an email security product that protects hosting servers against spam, phishing, and other email-based threats.

Imunify Email filters mail on about 5,000 hosting servers. Today the filter is rspamd with rules and a scoring model we train in-house. We are replacing it with a transformer we trained ourselves that runs on the customer's own CPU in under 100 MB and already beats rspamd at every operating point we measured. We are looking for a Senior Machine Learning Engineer to join our team and work alongside the engineer currently responsible for both the existing spam-filtering pipeline and the new ML model. Together, you will drive the development, improvement, and production rollout of our email security technology.

#7183. Final accreditation rule sets the stage for system overhaul

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The U.S. Department of Education said Thursday that it made “no substantive changes” to its August draft regulations.

The Education Department said it made “no substantive changes” to the draft rule it released in August and largely retained the language agreed upon with a committee of higher education representatives in the spring. The outcome of that process, known as negotiated rulemaking, bound the agency to much of the approved text.

The final rule, set to publish Friday in the Federal Register, will ensure accrediting agencies “prioritize diversity of thought on campuses,” the agency said.

When evaluating a college, accreditors must consider if the institution has academic freedom and freedom of inquiry protections, as well as policies “designed to support, promote, and appropriately prioritize intellectual diversity and the free exchange of ideas amongst faculty,” per the final rule.

But the Education Department asserted, “contrary to the hyperbole exercised by several commenters,” the new regulations do not force accreditors to adopt specific standards in exchange for federal recognition.

An accreditor that doesn’t adopt a formal policy on academic freedom or intellectual diversity will not be found out of federal compliance solely on either basis, according to the final regulations.

“Rather, an accrediting agency must consider relevant institutional policies and practices in the context of its existing standards and the institution's mission,” the Education Department said. “Where an agency has adopted standards addressing these matters, it must apply and enforce those standards consistently.”

Under Secretary of Education Nicholas Kent said Thursday that the final rule creates “a more competitive and transparent accreditation system” and increases accountability for colleges.

“For too long, accreditors have effectively served as a cartel to protect their interests, rather than the educational value that institutions of higher education are expected to deliver for students,” he said in a statement.

The Education Department accepted public comment on the rule for a month, the minimum window required by law. During that period, which the agency said “afforded the public a substantial opportunity for meaningful input,” it received some 7,000 responses.

Among them were requests by commenters to phase in the new accreditation regulations. The department rejected this request, saying “the eight months between November 2026 and July 2027 is sufficient time to update standards, policies, and procedures to achieve compliance.”

On Thursday, the Council for Higher Education Accreditation expressed disappointment with the final rule.

"Many of the central concerns raised by CHEA and the higher education community remain largely unresolved," Nasser Paydar, the organization's president, said in a statement. "Some of the provisions impose new requirements on accrediting organizations and institutions and prescribe aspects of how independent accrediting organizations carry out their work."

Paydar also highlighted concerns around possible impacts on institutional academic freedom, as well as the potential for increased regulatory burdens and costs.

are obsessive about tying out numbers to the penny

are known as the go-to person for tackling tough problems

are focused on polish, detail and quality

are a fast learner, frequently picking up new tips, tricks, and skills

Hands-on experience running both US and international payroll, including Canada and/or the EU

Experience with multi-state US payroll for a remote, distributed team

Strong experience working within PEO and/or EOR structures

Familiarity with startup-focused platforms such as Rippling, Deel, Remote, or similar

Comfort working independently as the subject-matter expert, juggling many moving pieces and flagging tax, legal, and compliance risk before it becomes a problem

CPP, FPC, or a similar payroll certification

Experience moving payroll in-house, switching providers, or migrating an HRIS

Experience with equity administration in Carta

Experience at a fast-growing company with a distributed, international team

You've personally caught a payroll error before anyone else did, and you still think about it fondly

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

#7165. Inside the University of Oregon’s plan to save $65 million a year

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Under a new proposal, the beleaguered public flagship would cut 18 academic programs and over 100 jobs, among other changes.

The University of Oregon is considering a proposal that would eliminate over 100 staff positions — just a third of which are vacant — and nearly 20 academic programs amid its efforts to resolve a projected structural deficit of $65 million.

The plan, presented by UO President Karl Scholz to the university’s faculty senate on Wednesday, would also merge or consolidate several academic departments.

Scholz said the latest proposal includes average cuts of 7.25% across all administrative and academic units, down from the 10% that had been forecasted over the summer. That comes out to $17.6 million in administrative services savings and $25.8 million in reduced academic costs, according to the plan.

“We will not be implementing these budget cuts across the board but instead will be applying the actions strategically to minimize the impact as much as possible on essential campus services,” he said in a campus letter Wednesday.

The university is taking campus comment on the plan, which is still in its recommendation phase, and will hold a series of listening sessions. Scholz will also meet with UO’s vice presidents in November before he makes a final decision on the proposal in early December.

Under the budget plan, UO would eliminate 18 graduate-level academic programs, most of which are in liberal arts fields. It noted that each program on the chopping block has had few graduates in recent years.

And one program, the business school’s Ph.D. in Management, has already paused admissions “because the faculty do not feel it is serving students well,” the proposal said. It averaged fewer than two degrees annually over the last three years.

Affected programs would stop enrolling new students effective fall 2027 and conclude once their current cohorts finish their degrees. The average master’s program would conclude its teach-out by spring 2028, and closing Ph.D. programs would typically shutter by spring 2032.

Central to many of the proposed cuts is the cost of graduate student-employees. Graduate students awarded GE support get a full tuition waiver and stipend in exchange for filling a research or teaching role at the university, according to UO’s website.

Most doctoral students receive research appointments that are externally funded. But some graduate students are supported through teaching appointments paid by UO.

“GE appointments represent a substantial recurring expenditure,” the proposal said. It seeks to bring the cost of graduate funding “into alignment with available resources” and preserve appointments for departments “where graduate students contribute most significantly to faculty research and scholarship.”

That means focusing GE funding on doctoral training and research, where ongoing support “is central to the degree model,” it said.

Those priorities mean a significant shift away from supporting graduate students in the university’s College of Arts and Sciences. Under the plan, the college would reduce the number of graduate students it supports by up to 40%.

Other programs are restricting their admissions to avoid doling out as much financial support for graduate students.

If approved, the university would pause recruitment for four Ph.D. programs — Architecture; Landscape Architecture; History of Art and Architecture; and Planning, Public Policy and Management — while it conducts cost-benefit analyses and aligns GE-supported admissions “with college priorities and budget.”

Similarly, UO would accept at most five students a year into its art MFA program to limit costly admissions.

The university plan said it will honor the GE support to which it has already committed.

UO is also eyeing several organizational consolidations, including combining its Classics and Religious Studies departments. The merger would “eliminate duplicate leadership responsibilities and reduce faculty service and administrative demands,” the plan said.

The university would similarly combine its earth sciences and geography departments under the proposal. And it would merge its design college with its school of music and dance.

Three undergraduate literature departments — German and Scandinavian Studies; Romance Languages; and Russian, East European, and Eurasian Studies — could also be consolidated into one academic unit.

If approved, department mergers in the design college would be complete by next fall. Department mergers in all other colleges would be expected to be in place by fall 2028.

The university’s libraries, which UO classifies as an administrative unit overseen by the provost, would see a 5% budget reduction under the plan and could lose up to eight full-time positions.

If the university approves the proposed budget plan in December, the resulting job cuts would be its second round of significant workforce reductions in less than two years. In 2025, UO laid off some 120 employees and cut about 60 vacant positions.

Several converging factors, most of which center on enrollment, have resulted in UO’s budget pinch, according to Scholz.

Oregon's high school graduate population is expected to drop about 19%, outpacing the national forecast of a 13% decrease according to data from the Western Interstate Commission for Higher Education.

Scholz cited increased competition with peer universities for students and a significant loss of out-of-state enrollment. Nearly half of UO’s students, 47.1%, are from outside of Oregon, a notable share for a public flagship. And the university’s tuition sticker price for out-of-state undergraduates is more than double when it lists for in-state students.

The university also faces a politically engineered drop in international enrollment.

“These dynamics are not only affecting the University of Oregon,” Scholz said. “They represent a changing reality across higher education and are likely to be the new market environment to which we must adjust.”

At the start of 2026, Oregon’s Higher Education Coordinating Commission said all seven public universities are facing shrinking pools of potential students, declining enrollment and constrained state funding.

#7147. Container Engineer at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth. More than 9 million developers and 250,000+ customers now build on Supabase, and the majority of new databases on the platform are created by AI app builders — so the systems underneath have to stay reliable while they grow fast.

Container Engineering (ConEng) owns the internal container platform that runs Supabase's multi-tenant, multi-region services — the managed Kubernetes platform that other engineering teams build and deploy on top of. We own cluster lifecycle and cluster classes, multi-cluster and multi-region topology, capacity, scheduling, isolation and runtime safety, the higher-level platform APIs, the Kubernetes deployment contract and Argo semantics, rollout primitives, and runtime configuration and secret injection.

Your job is to make the platform itself trustworthy, observable, and cheap to operate at scale — and to raise the reliability bar for everyone who depends on it. This is not a gatekeeper role. You'll make the reliable path the easy path: building the automation and platform APIs that let product teams run safely by default, instrumenting what we operate, and making sure that when something breaks we detect it and recover fast.

Tooling & ecosystem — deep knowledge of the container and orchestration landscape (Kubernetes, containerd/OCI, the CNCF ecosystem) and a clear point of view on how to compose it.

Software engineering — designing and building the platform's automation, controllers, and internal tooling as production code, including solutions that don't come pre-shaped by an existing tool.

Operational / SRE practice — the discipline (SLOs, error budgets, incident response, ORRs) that runs the platform at scale as a predictable one.

We expect you to be genuinely deep in at least one of these, and many of our strongest candidates are deep in two (usually leaning toward one). Bringing real strength across all three is rare and exceptional — and even then, no one is equally strong in all three at once, so you'd focus where you're most needed at any given time. Different profiles are welcome here; tell us which of these is your center of gravity.

Regardless of the skill set, all team members carry a share of the team's on-call rota, responding to platform incidents and driving them to resolution and follow-up.

Define SLIs/SLOs and error budgets for the container platform itself, and build error-budget policies that actually gate platform changes.

Own the Operational Readiness Review (ORR) process for new platform capabilities and major changes.

Design and track metrics for platform uptime, and increase observability into the platform by capturing the right metrics, logs, and traces across the platform layers.

Drive the incident-to-improvement pipeline: facilitate blameless postmortems, connect findings to readiness gaps, spot repeat failure patterns, and push through systemic fixes.

Build and maintain the platform's automation, controllers, operators, and internal tooling — writing production code and shipping it, not just wiring together existing tools.

Design and implement the platform-side CI/CD and rollout primitives that speed up platform deployments while working within proper change and release management.

Implement and maintain security automation for the platform: intrusion detection, automated remediation, and patch management.

Identify and quantify operational toil, and build the automation that removes it rather than just documenting it.

Contribute to the platform's continual testing automation — the test suites and pipelines that validate platform releases are fit for purpose before they reach tenants.

Act as a platform reliability and architecture expert — contribute to failure-mode analysis, dependency mapping, and resilience design for multi-tenant container workloads.

Evaluate and evolve the platform's tooling ecosystem (orchestration, runtime, networking, isolation, and observability stack) with a clear, opinionated view on trade-offs.

Improve system architecture to reduce infrastructure cost while balancing security, reliability, and performance across tenants.

Guide teams building on the platform to design sustainable on-call practices for their own services: alert quality, escalation paths, runbook coverage, and noise reduction.

Have 5+ years in platform engineering, container/Kubernetes platform operations, SRE, or production infrastructure, and have operated production systems at scale and carried on-call for them.

Are genuinely deep in at least one — ideally two — of the three strength areas below (and tell us which is your center of gravity):

Tooling & ecosystem — know the container/orchestration landscape well: Kubernetes, containerd and the OCI/CRI standards, and the surrounding CNCF ecosystem (e.g. Argo CD/Argo Rollouts for GitOps and progressive delivery; Cilium/eBPF networking and the Gateway API; Karpenter/KEDA-style autoscaling; and workload-isolation runtimes such as gVisor or Kata Containers). You reason about trade-offs rather than reaching for defaults.

Software engineering — write real production code (Go and/or a comparable systems language) and can build controllers, operators, and platform APIs — stepping back to the underlying problem and building the right solution when no off-the-shelf tool fits.

Operational / SRE practice — are fluent in SLOs, error budgets, and operational KPIs; have led incident response and blameless postmortems; and have driven down MTTD/MTTR for systems you operated.

Have worked with large-scale multi-tenant systems and understand isolation as a spectrum — from namespaces, RBAC, network policies, and resource quotas through to stronger primitives such as virtual clusters (e.g. vCluster) or VM-class runtimes.

Operate confidently on AWS (multiple accounts, IAM, VPC) and are comfortable with infrastructure-as-code (Pulumi preferred; Terraform/CDK also fine) and Kubernetes.

Script and automate to eliminate toil rather than absorb it.

#7138. How a university partnership is bringing public procurement into the talent pipeline

Colleges and universities face an ongoing challenge: helping students connect what they learn in the classroom with career opportunities that they may not know exist. Public procurement may not be an obvious career choice for students studying business, supply chain management, or public administration, but introducing students to the profession can start long before graduation, including through their coursework, workforce experiences and procurement professionals they meet along the way.

NASPO is working with Arizona State University (ASU) to create more of those opportunities. A key part of the partnership is the naming of the NASPO Department of Supply Chain Management at ASU, the first academic department of its kind that explicitly includes public procurement in its curriculum. The department was recently ranked the #1 undergraduate supply chain management program in the nation by U.S. News & World Report. Supported by a landmark $35 million gift from NASPO, the department is creating a dedicated hub for education, research and innovation in public procurement. The work will support students preparing to enter the field while giving current public procurement professionals opportunities to strengthen their skills and contribute to initiatives that advance public procurement nationwide.

A partnership with a university means understanding more than its academic programs. For NASPO, it means starting with understanding a student’s perspective and world. What are they learning? What questions are faculty discussing with their classes? Where could exploring public procurement careers add a new perspective to the conversation? To keep that engagement consistent, NASPO has created a liaison position at Arizona State University.

As Program Operations Coordinator, Mia Carlton works directly with the university community, giving NASPO a closer look at the ideas, interests and priorities taking shape on campus. This role helps NASPO find more natural ways to bring public procurement into students’ academic experiences through coursework, research projects and campus events, while connecting state members to speaking, mentoring and collaboration opportunities on campus and within their own states. This continued engagement has helped NASPO and ASU identify opportunities to learn from one another and strengthen the relationship over time.

“Most juniors and seniors only know what procurement is through the lens of private companies without giving thought toward public entities,” Carlton said. “In just two months of on-campus speaking engagements and career fairs, I have students reaching out regularly about learning more and getting involved in the public sector. They are deeply drawn to the impact one can make in their community.”

Introducing students to public procurement begins with helping them understand what the profession looks like and where it can lead. NASPO Ignite is NASPO’s talent development initiative that supports creating opportunities to make those introductions, both inside and outside the classroom at every school level. NASPO Ignite also reaches high school students through a partnership with DECA. The collaboration introduces younger students to the procurement profession through speaking opportunities, judging and the DECA NASPO Procurement Challenge, which allows students to step into the shoes of a procurement professional in their community.

Beyond coursework, college students can gain hands-on experience through NASPO’s Internship Connect Program, which connects state procurement offices with collegiate talent. Through the program, NASPO recruits students for participating state offices and provides grant funding to support their internships. During the summer program, students have the opportunity to work in selected participating state and territorial procurement offices, with the program open to all 50 states and U.S. territories. Through these experiences, students gain firsthand insight into public procurement and the role it plays in serving communities.

NASPO also offers an Academic Scholarship to support students who show an interest in public procurement or public service. The scholarship program helps reduce some of the financial barriers students may face while pursuing their education. By providing support as students study and complete their degrees, the program gives recipients more opportunity to focus on their coursework and explore their professional interests, while encouraging them to continue developing that interest throughout their academic journey.

The NASPO-ASU partnership demonstrates how public procurement can be integrated into the higher education experience through relationships with faculty, research opportunities and direct engagement with the profession.

“Having NASPO connected directly to campus allows us to bring public procurement into the conversations already happening in classrooms and across the university,” said Adegoke Oke, Professor of Supply Chain Management and NASPO Endowed Department Chair, NASPO Department of Supply Chain Management, W.P. Carey School of Business at Arizona State University. “We can connect what students are learning with the challenges procurement professionals are navigating every day, while creating opportunities for faculty, students and state procurement offices to learn from one another. That exchange of knowledge and experience is what makes this partnership so valuable.”

Building on this approach, NASPO’s newly expanded Academic Talent Expansion Program will empower more state procurement offices to partner with colleges and universities in their communities. Through internships, scholarships, career fairs, guest speakers, research collaborations and other opportunities, states can introduce students to public procurement while building relationships with institutions that can help develop the future workforce.

Together with NASPO Ignite and its other talent development initiatives, these efforts reflect NASPO’s continued commitment to helping students discover public procurement and giving state procurement offices more ways to connect with emerging talent.

For more information about NASPO Ignite and its initiatives, email [email protected] or visit our website at naspoignite.org

#7136. Week In Review: What makes an attendance strategy successful?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

As schools work to reduce their student chronic absenteeism rates, two recent reports highlight which strategies are the most and least effective in improving attendance. For instance, the reports concluded that attendance efforts that strongly rely on truancy laws to get students back in classrooms are less effective than approaches that focus on other strategies like building family engagement and fostering student connectedness.

Additionally, school leaders using multiple evidence-based strategies at once to tackle chronic absenteeism saw more success than principals who only used one effective strategy, according to an analysis of principal surveys by the Everyone Graduates Center, Partnership for Student Success and the GRAD Partnership.

The percentage increase in the number of special education state complaints nationwide between the 2023-24 and 2024-25 school years, according to an analysis released in October by the Center for Appropriate Dispute Resolution in Special Education. There were 10,610 written state complaints filed in 2024-25 — rising from 9,927 the year prior, found CADRE, a federally funded technical assistance center. Over the last decade, meanwhile, the total state complaints surged by 113% between 2014 and 2024.

CTE kind of stands on its own right now. We have a lot of support from both sides of the aisle when it comes to CTE.

Assistant superintendent for business and operations, Western Maricopa Education Center

While many public schools nationwide continue to struggle with shrinking enrollment and teacher shortages for hard-to-staff roles, Arizona's Western Maricopa Education Center is trying to expand and keep up with growing student demand. On top of that, the career and technical education district has almost no CTE teacher vacancies, French said, crediting the district’s competitive salaries. He added that the growing public support for CTE is helping the district invest and build more campuses in high demand industries like healthcare and technology.

#7134. Chief Customer Officer at Xapo Bank

Diversity is at the heart of who we are at Xapo Bank. We’re a fully distributed team of over 160 Xapiens that work remotely from 50+ countries around the world.

Our beginning: A world that enjoys economic freedom and wealth protection,no matter where you live or who is running your country.To achieve that, we search the world for the best people for the job. We work hard, think globally, and inspire each other to learn and grow. We are committed to changing the way things are done.

Although we are headquartered in Gibraltar, this is a full time, 100% remote position Work from anywhere!

Xapo's product suite has grown from a Bitcoin vault into a full member proposition: BTC and USD accounts that pay interest, a global card with Bitcoin cashback, Bitcoin-backed loans, a growing suite of BTC wealth and credit products, and access to stocks, ETFs, and stablecoins, all inside one regulated relationship.

Xapo is a bank, and a bank earns on the money its members choose to hold with it. Each of those products shipped well. What hasn't yet existed is a single owner of the economics of the customer relationship: one person accountable for winning the right members, growing what they hold with us, and keeping them, and for the balance-sheet products that make that possible.

The Chief Customer Officer is a new role at the top of the house, reporting to the CEO. You will run the stream that grows what members hold with Xapo, and you will own the Customer P&L, retail and institutional. It brings growth, client coverage, product management, and product design under one leader who is judged on customer contribution, the assets attracted and kept, and the members who stay. This is a customer-focused role in the most commercial sense: what a member earns from us, not the feature we shipped, is the centre of every decision.

The measure of success is a Customer P&L that grows on what members hold and keep: LTV:CAC improving, approved-to-paid rising, churn falling, and customer contribution the business can plan against.

This is a banking role, not a requirements-gathering role, a marketing role, or a role you can run from a dashboard. Products are priced on the balance sheet and judged on the money they attract and keep.

This part matters as much as the mandate. The known failure mode for this role, in any company, is a CCO with a polished customer-insight deck and no ownership of the number. That is disqualifying here.

Day 30: The Customer P&L baseline is documented across retail and institutional: customer contribution, LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, net paid-member growth, and assets attracted and kept. You have formed your own independent view of where members are won, lost, and under-served, and shared it with the CEO and Board.Day 60: The unified Customer operating model is live: one set of key results across Growth, Client Coverage, Mission PMs, and Product Design, read weekly from one source. Every mission PM owns a product's contribution and its price, and the roadmap is ranked on what it earns, built with Finance and the line leads.Day 90: At least one measurable improvement in LTV:CAC, approved-to-paid, churn, or net new AUM flow is live and attributable to a change you made, not a feature ship, an outcome moved. Every cell in your line has defined key results and an owner who explains the number every week.

Shape the FutureImprove lives through cutting-edge technology, work 100% remotely from anywhere in the world.

Great work-life balanceBuild amazing things with a balance of autonomy and collaborative teamwork. Set your own work schedule and make use of a flexible PTO plan when you need to recharge.

Expect ExcellenceCollaborate, learn, and grow with a high-performance team. Learn how you learn best - from books to conferences, you’ll get a yearly budget for your individual learning and development goals.

At Xapo, we prioritize consumer protection and adhere to regulatory requirements by ensuring that all Xapiens are accountable for upholding principles of fair treatment, transparency, and ethical conduct in their interactions with customers and stakeholders.

The cost of the deferred maintenance backlog faced by Louisiana’s public colleges. A report from Gordian, a building intelligence firm, to the state’s board of regents found that eliminating the backlog and addressing forthcoming maintenance needs will take $777 million in annual capital spending over the next decade.

“For too long, accreditors have effectively served as a cartel to protect their interests, rather than the educational value that institutions of higher education are expected to deliver for students.”

The U.S. Department of Education last week released the final version of its accreditation rule, setting up a significant overhaul of the longstanding process. Under the regulations, which take effect in July, accreditors will be required to consider colleges’ intellectual diversity policies and cannot enforce “illegal diversity, equity, and inclusion requirements” as defined by the Trump administration.

#7124. To grow teacher capacity, measure what matters

When I was a school principal, I’d put aside several hours every week for one-on-one coaching with teachers. I did it because I’d seen what happened as teachers grew in their professional capacities: they were more satisfied and more likely to stay, students were more engaged, and stakeholder relationships improved. For this reason, I’ve spent most of my career supporting educators’ professional growth.

Effectively cultivating teacher growth requires focusing on behaviors proven to enhance professional practice. Through my work with a school improvement organization, I observed significant alignment between my coaching experiences and the five overarching dimensions of professional practice identified in the organization’s research. Those dimensions and the related factors that strengthen and sustain quality instructional practice include:

Once school leaders know what to measure, they need to choose data sources that align with the established growth goals. Below are some useful options:

There’s no exact formula for deciding which practice should top the improvement priority list. However, that decision should not rest solely with school leaders. Instead, allow teachers to review their own data and determine, through discussion and reflection, which dimension and action deserve their focus.

A simple set of questions can guide the conversation:

While a teacher may need to grow in all five dimensions, no one can work on all five at once. If all data sources point to the same issue, that’s an obvious priority.

Without data, educators will struggle to understand exactly where they’ve improved and by how much. But when schools measure teacher practices within the five dimensions and act on the data, they build a culture of growth for staff and students alike.

Nicole Reeves, Ed.D., serves as vice president of professional learning at Cognia, a global nonprofit organization. Prior to this role, she was a teacher, assistant principal, principal, national trainer, and educational consultant. As an educational expert and facilitator, she has supported administrators, teachers, and board-level personnel at all levels to improve organizational and instructional processes that support student learning.

#7118. A look at the eye-popping endowment gains at Harvard, WashU, Stanford and beyond

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Several wealthy universities reported returns over 20% amid uncertainty in both higher ed and the economy writ large.

In the 2026 fiscal year, rich colleges got even richer.

Harvard University may have brought in $123 million less in federal research funding as it became the favorite higher-education punching bag of the Trump administration. But it still reported a surplus for the year thanks in large part to its endowment.

Last fiscal year, which ended June 30 for Harvard and many other colleges, the Ivy League institution’s endowment grew at a rate of just over 27% to $68 billion. That growth translated to $11 billion in real dollars, a figure that represents more — typically much more — money than is found in nearly every other endowment in the country but for the largest 20 or so. And it helped Harvard fund $400 in research activity out of pocket.

Harvard is also facing newly increased federal taxes on endowment income, which went into effect for the wealthiest colleges in January. The university's leaders are budgeting for $300 million a year in federal taxes on its investment income as a result of the change.

Harvard may be in a class of its own in dollar terms. But many college endowments across the country have reported double digit growth as equity and other investment markets boomed amid persistent inflation, sluggish job growth, the ongoing U.S. war in Iran, high energy prices and widespread economic and geopolitical uncertainty.

Such growth is a bright spot — for those lucky enough to have started with some meat to their endowments — in a higher ed world under pressure by demographic shifts and myriad other factors.

In a September report, S&P Global Ratings analysts said they had noticed “an increasing number of institutions relying on elevated endowment draws” and other uses of the endowment amid diminishing net tuition revenue. That’s a risky gamble, they noted, one that trades short-term liquidity for long-term financial security.

Those colleges were the exception, though. “Most have focused on building their endowments and preserving resources amid uncertainty,” the analysts added.

Here’s a look at some of the biggest endowment winners and their year-over-year returns from 2026 fiscal:

#7112. Choosing digital curriculum that works for every learner

Today's classrooms are more diverse than ever. Students bring different strengths, abilities, backgrounds, interests and preferences to every learning experience. As digital learning continues to expand, curriculum providers face an important challenge: designing courses that meet the needs of all learners, not just some.

When evaluating digital curriculum, five pillars define course quality: standards alignment, audience-centered design, data-informed decisions, accessibility and continuous improvement.

One of the most revealing questions educators can ask during the evaluation process is simple: Who was this course designed for?

If the answer is "every learner," accessibility can't be an afterthought. It must be built into the curriculum from the start. Increasingly, schools are asking whether a course can truly be considered high quality if every learner cannot fully engage with it.

More than 8 million students in the United States receive special education services and many use assistive technologies as part of their everyday learning experience. Some may rely on screen readers, text magnification tools, speech-to-text software or adaptive devices. Others benefit from options that allow them to customize how they interact with content and demonstrate mastery.

Yet accessible design extends well beyond students with identified disabilities.

An English learner may benefit from listening to content while following along visually. A student recovering from a concussion may need alternatives to text-heavy materials. A student accessing coursework from multiple devices may benefit from responsive design and intuitive navigation. Features originally designed to improve accessibility often enhance the learning experience for all students.

In practice, accessible digital curriculum incorporates features that make learning more flexible and intuitive for a broad range of students, including:

Closed captioning and transcripts for multimedia content

Many of these practices align with the principles of Universal Design for Learning (UDL), which encourages educators to provide multiple ways for students to engage with content, process information and demonstrate understanding. Rather than designing for a single type of learner, UDL recognizes that flexibility benefits all students and should be incorporated into learning experiences from the beginning.

For district leaders, accessibility is also a strong indicator of a curriculum provider's overall commitment to quality. Providers that prioritize accessible design are often demonstrating a broader investment in learner-centered development, thoughtful user experience and continuous improvement.

With a growing number of digital curriculum options available, identifying the right solution can feel overwhelming. To help education leaders navigate the evaluation process, we developed a free, comprehensive Digital Curriculum Buyer's Guide that explores key indicators of quality, including accessibility, instructional design and alignment to student needs. Drawing on more than 25 years of experience in online education and curriculum development, the guide provides practical strategies and questions to help districts make informed decisions.

#7103. Inside the Supreme Court case that could decide Title IX employee protections

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

This month, the high court will hear arguments on whether higher education workers have the right to sue their institutions for sex-based discrimination.

A critical question facing the U.S. Supreme Court in its 2026-27 term is whether employees of federally funded educational institutions can sue their employers for sex discrimination under Title IX.

On Nov. 30, the justices will hear arguments in Crowther v. Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia, a case that experts agree will have big implications on future litigation brought by employees against colleges and universities.

Georgia Institute of Technology, more commonly known as Georgia Tech, fired its head women’s basketball coach MaChelle Joseph in 2019, less than two months after she filed an internal complaint alleging that the university was discriminating against her and her sports program by not providing the same resources that it did for the men’s basketball team.

The university countered that it terminated Joseph following an independent investigation that revealed certain players felt bullied by her.

In 2020, Augusta University — also part of the University System of Georgia — reassigned and later decided against renewing the contract of fine arts professor Thomas Crowther after a Title IX investigation into an anonymous sexual harassment complaint. Crowther claimed that the university ignored numerous witness statements in his favor.

Both Crowther and Joseph sued the system’s regent board alleging sex discrimination under Title IX, which prohibits sex-based discrimination in federally funded education programs. The Atlanta-based 11th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals consolidated the cases and dismissed both Title IX claims, finding the 1972 law did not provide individuals with the right to sue for sex discrimination in employment.

Most other federal appellate courts disagree, with eight circuit courts having ruled that Title IX permits employees to sue the institutions that employ or employed them. The 5th and 7th Circuits are the other appellate courts that have blocked such lawsuits.

But how the Supreme Court will handle this case is far from clear.

This won’t be the first time the Supreme Court has ruled on Title IX employee claims. The high court ruled 5-4 in 2005 that an Alabama high school girls’ basketball coach and gym teacher could proceed with his Title IX lawsuit in Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education.

Roderick Jackson had complained to supervisors about the unequal funding and access to facilities and equipment his team received but was allegedly ignored. He began receiving negative performance reviews and ultimately lost his job as the girls’ coach though he remained employed as a teacher. He argued officials retaliated against him in violation of Title IX for reporting sex-based discrimination.

To Miriam Becker-Cohen, the Supreme Court’s holding for Jackson should make Crowther a rather easy case. Becker-Cohen is senior appellate counsel at the Constitutional Accountability Center, a nonprofit law firm and think tank, and is the lead author of an amicus brief supporting the plaintiffs.

There is no limitation on who can bring a private lawsuit in any Title IX case, according to Becker-Cohen. “Jackson only makes that case even stronger because Jackson involved a school employee,” she said.

If anything, Becker-Cohen contended that Jackson was a harder case because the court had to resolve the issue of whether it involved intentional sex discrimination. But there is “no question” the acts complained of in Crowther are intentional sex discrimination, she said.

But Jon Israel, a management-side sports and employment law attorney with Foley & Lardner in New York, suggested the cases have differences even though both involve coaches.

The finding in Jackson of an implied right to sue “makes it a little more distinguishable” than the Crowther case, which is dealing with a direct right to sue for nonstudents at higher educational institutions.

Gregory Garre, who served as U.S. solicitor general during the George W. Bush administration and now practices with Latham & Watkins, agrees there is a distinction. Garre filed an amicus brief in the case on behalf of the National School Attorneys Association supporting the University System of Georgia’s regents board.

“When it comes to Title IX, what they’re asking the court to do here is really dramatically expand the right from its traditional focus, which is on sex discrimination against students, into the realm of employment discrimination against employees of schools,” Garre told Higher Ed Dive.

Garre added that Title VII of the Civil Rights Act, which Congress amended shortly before passing Title IX, applies to employees of educational institutions as well as other organizations, meaning they can sue over sex-based employment discrimination under that statute. And, he called that timing significant.

“The statutory history here is a pretty compelling indication that Congress did not intend for Title IX to cover employment discrimination because Congress had passed Title VII [amendments] just months before it enacted Title IX.”

As a result, both Garre and Israel have said they think Title VII provides ample recourse for employees, such as those in the Crowther case.

But Becker-Cohen disagreed. “At least for some employees of federally funded institutions, Title VII isn’t going to be an option,” she said.

While the remedies may overlap, Becker-Cohen said Title VII has a much tighter statute of limitations, which can be as short as 180 days, than Title IX, which has no set limitations period — so courts rely on the most analogous state law limitations period.

She also said Title VII requires individuals to exhaust other avenues, such as filing a complaint with the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission before they can sue, among other procedural roadblocks not present in Title IX.

And that’s not all, according to sports law professor Barbara Osborne, who has represented colleges and universities on compliance issues.

“Title VII is strictly based on the sex of the employee. So that would not include what the typical Title IX claims are. They are different causes of action,” said Osborne, who teaches at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill.

In the typical Title IX employment case, she explained, the person suffering the negative employment action is not necessarily discriminated against because of their sex but rather for complaining about sex discrimination in the athletics program, which she noted that both Jackson and Joseph — the former Georgia Tech coach — clearly did.

Osborne also pointed out that Title IX regulations contain a section related to employment. “Why was it necessary to create regulations related to employment if there weren’t situations within education that might be different than normal employment situations?”

The Supreme Court could have said Title IX remedies were not available for employment retaliation in Jackson and declined to do so, Osborne and Becker-Cohen both were quick to note.

But more than two decades later, Crowther comes before a much different court, with the author of the Jackson opinion, former Justice Sandra Day O’Connor, and others in the majority, having long since retired or died.

College athletes are not considered employees. But that day could be coming in light of the massive changes affecting college sports in recent years, including the influx of name, image and likeness money.

As a result, how the Supreme Court resolves Crowther could affect college athletes themselves should they someday be deemed employees of the institutions whose teams they play on.

“If Title IX includes employees, the class of potential Title IX plaintiffs is going to be expanded dramatically,” said Garre, adding that the possibility of college athletes being considered employees would “certainly create new wrinkles” in litigation.

One of those wrinkles for college-athletes-as-employees could become a case of “be careful what you wish for” according to Israel, if the Court rules against the plaintiffs.

“If the student-athletes are treated as employees, and Crowther decides that Title VII is the exclusive remedy for employees then they’re in that Title VII world — not Title IX.” Israel said.

He contended that some disparate treatment arguments — that an institution is treating female athletes differently than male athletes playing revenue-producing sports, such as football or basketball — might effectively disappear under Title VII as a result.

According to Israel, that’s because a college gymnast’s work, for instance, differs from that of a football player so they would not be similarly situated under Title VII. “If you put it in a different context, a line worker versus an executive, they wouldn’t have the same compensation,” the sports law attorney said.

“The job description of a student-athlete would be virtually identical no matter what team they participate on, right?” she said. “They’re going to be expected to go to class. They’re going to be expected to go to practice, follow the nutrition program and whatever else. The job description isn’t (to) be able to perform in front of 15,000 fans. That’s not what the job is.”

Becker-Cohen acknowledged that if the Supreme Court were to rule for the system’s regents board, it would leave student-athletes and others with no remedy for sex discrimination unless they could make out a claim under Title VII.

“To the extent that the Court carves out college employees from the reach of Title IX’s private right of action, if the class of individuals who are considered college employees increases, that will just harm more people,” Becker-Cohen said.

Correction: The subtitle in a previous version of this story misstated which month the Supreme Court would hear oral arguments in the case. This has been updated.

#7089. Abrupt leadership departures at two private universities

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Bard College announced its next leader amid ongoing scrutiny. Plus, a Colorado president will step down after two decades at her university.

Editor’s note: The Leadership Ledger is a monthly roundup of some of the most noteworthy college leadership changes nationwide.

In a month of tricks and treats, colleges across the U.S. got their share of both.

Two public university systems, in Georgia and Wisconsin, announced new leaders following much-discussed departures earlier this year. Conversely, two private universities saw their president abruptly resign in October, with one providing little insight into what prompted the leadership change.

From Texas to Wisconsin, we’re rounding up some of last month’s biggest leadership changes below.

President: Thomas Fanning Institution: University System of Georgia Coming or going? Coming

The University System of Georgia’s governing board on Oct. 1 confirmed former utility executive Thomas Fanning as its next chancellor. Fanning, the sole finalist for the job, is to take office Jan. 1, replacing Sonny Perdue. This will be the first position in higher education for Fanning, who spent more than 40 years in leadership positions at Southern Co. before retiring in 2023.

USG has a history of hiring leaders from outside of academia. Perdue, who announced his intention to retire in April, had a decades-long political career prior to taking the helm of USG in 2022. During his four-year tenure, he installed 14 presidents across the system’s 25 colleges, and USG’s fall enrollment repeatedly broke records. Perdue’s last day is set for Dec. 31.

President: Walter Iwanenko Institution: Gannon University Coming or going? Going

Walter Iwanenko stepped down as president of Gannon University, effective Oct. 8, the Pennsylvania institution announced the next day.

Gannon is in the process of acquiring nearby Ursuline College, a restructuring that Iwanenko said he committed to seeing the university through. The merger of the two Catholic institutions is expected to be complete by mid-December.

“Now that it is nearly done, I am stepping down to pursue other opportunities,” he said in a statement.

Gannon’s board chose Keith Taylor, who previously led the university for over a decade, to replace Iwanenko starting Oct. 8. It indicated Taylor’s appointment would not be a long-term one, saying he would assist the university “through this transition and the search for a new permanent president.”

Shortly after he took office, Taylor said Gannon is preparing for a reduction in force amid a sharp decline in enrollment and a broader institutional restructuring, Erie News Now reported.

President: Daniel Pullin Institution: Texas Christian University Coming or going? Going

Texas Christian University Chancellor Daniel Pullin abruptly resigned on Oct. 22 after less than two years in the role. The announcement came via a campus email sent by Victor Boschini, who headed TCU for over two decades before departing in 2025. Boschini is once again leading TCU after its board appointed him to replace Pullin.

The email did not give a reason for Pullin’s departure.

President: Amy Parsons Institution: Colorado State University Coming or going? Going

Amy Parsons will step down as president of Colorado State University, she said in an Oct. 28 campus letter. Her last day will be Dec. 31.

Parsons, an alumna of CSU, became the public institution's president in 2023 after holding senior leadership positions within the CSU system for nearly two decades.

In an Oct. 28 statement, CSU System Chancellor Tony Frank touted the university’s improved national visibility and the student access initiatives established during Parsons’ tenure. He said the search for her successor will begin in the spring and be led by Rico Munn, the incoming CSU system chancellor who is set to start in mid-2027. Longtime faculty member Rick Miranda, who served as CSU’s interim president in 2022 prior to Parsons' hiring, will again be the university’s acting leader beginning in January.

Like many public institutions, CSU is in a tough place financially amid underwhelming state support. Colorado’s fiscal 2027 budget kept higher education funding flat while capping public colleges’ ability to raise tuition for in-state students at 3.5%. CSU is set to cut some $39 million from its budget next year through staff layoffs and other measures.

President: Marvin Krislov Institution: Bard College Coming or going? Coming

Marvin Krislov will be the next president of Bard College, the private New York institution’s board said on Oct. 29. Krislov currently leads Pace University and previously held the presidency at Oberlin College. Following a unanimous vote, Bard’s board signed Krislov to a three-year contract which will begin July 1.

The announcement comes as Bard continues to navigate leadership challenges and unwanted public attention.

Leon Botstein resigned as Bard president this summer over intense scrutiny focused on his professional relationship with Jeffrey Epstein and Epstein’s resulting involvement with Bard. Botstein had led the campus for five decades.

Jonathan Becker, a longtime Bard senior administrator, has served as acting president in the interim.

Initially, Botstein continued to hold positions at Bard, including a professorship. But he stepped down from those roles this month after Democratic lawmakers made fresh allegations that he may have directly participated in Epstein’s abuse and exploitation of his victims. Botstein denies those allegations.

President: Tonjanita Johnson Institution: Universities of Wisconsin Coming or going? Coming

Tonjanita Johnson will be the next president of the Universities of Wisconsin, following a unanimous vote from the system’s board on Oct. 30. Johnson, a senior administrator with the University of Alabama system, will take office at the start of the new year.

Johnson will be the system’s first permanent leader since trustees ousted its last president, Jay Rothman, in April.

Rothman had said previously that the board had given him an ultimatum — resign or retire by the end of the calendar year, or face termination by the board. He said he declined to step down because the board did not give him a reason for the request. The president of the board disputed this account, saying at the time that Rothman “was not without notice, nor was this process sudden.”

Senior leaders attributed its financial woes to “shifting enrollment trends, unpredictable state and federal funding, and rising costs.”

#7087. Low-income elementary math achievers underrepresented, NWEA finds

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Schools should treat access to math opportunities as an equity issue and provide more identification and learning opportunities, the report said.

The report stressed that equity challenges were especially pronounced in the elementary grades. A possible explanation, NWEA said, is that early elementary math achievement is especially sensitive to instructional opportunities, home environments and out-of-school supports.

In elementary school, students from higher-income families might have more access to supplemental learning opportunities, stable learning conditions, and advocacy for more rigorous coursework. Meanwhile, lower-income students may be less consistently identified and given less-challenging opportunities.

The report recommends treating advanced achievement as an equity issue. It suggests that schools and districts should:

“Our findings highlight that there are many lower-income students with the potential to score at the very top of the distribution in math,” said Megan Kuhfeld, NWEA's director of growth modeling and data analytics, in an Oct. 29 statement. “However, the challenge is whether we identify their ability early and then consistently provide the opportunities and support they need to sustain it.”

In 2023, an EdTrust and Just Equations study found stark gaps in access to advanced math courses in the higher grades among high-achieving students from different economic and racial backgrounds.

Black, Latino and low-income students were the least likely among high-achievers to enroll in Advanced Placement or International Baccalaureate calculus by the time they graduated high school. Asian and White students and students from higher-income backgrounds were more likely to participate in comparison.

In 2024, when the COVID-19 pandemic recovery efforts were in full swing, some 32% of 6,800 K-12 parents surveyed by Gallup said that financial barriers prevented their children from attending summer programs, one of the main avenues school districts used to address academic recovery and that was shown to benefit achievement.

#7085. New College of Florida eyes $47.5M in new loans

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The financing proposal will, in part, support the institution’s state-mandated takeover of the University of South Florida’s Sarasota-Manatee campus.

Of the new financing, $40 million would come from BankUnited, a regional financial institution, while another $7.5 million would come from an unnamed “accredited investor,” according to a project summary included in the board’s Tuesday meeting agenda.

Both loans would be secured with liens on New College revenue tied to the student center and other facilities as well as backing from the institution’s foundation, which has unrestricted assets of $3.6 million.

The financing package contains several exceptions to the State University System of Florida’s debt policies, requiring further approval from its board of governors. Following a New College board resolution unanimously passed on Tuesday, the institution will formally seek the governors’ approval of the financing at a meeting next week.

The loans represent private deals as opposed to a public bond offering, which SUSF typically requires its institutions to pursue. The project summary pointed to the short timeline for acquiring financing and the current bond market. “A private placement of the Debt was not only necessary but is expected to yield the best results in terms of cost and accommodative structure,” the document stated.

The smaller loan from the unnamed source also contains a subordinate lien on revenue, something only permitted by the system when necessary. The project summary said that the secondary lien was needed because of a dollar cap on the larger loan that necessitated a second loan.

The financing is also projected to fall below the system’s required thresholds for debt coverage — a measure of an institution’s ability to generate the cash needed to make debt service payments — until 2037.

The measure mandating New College’s acquisition of the USF campus required the former to lock down financing by this coming December. The primary cost of acquisition is tied to New College absorbing USF Sarasota-Manatee’s debt. And that debt is tied to Atala Hall, a six-story, 100,000-square-foot residence hall and activity center built in 2023.

That building, located less than a mile from New College’s campus, will be a prize for the smaller institution. Its first two floors function as a student union while the upper floors hold 200 beds of student housing.

“Acquiring Atala Hall will allow NCF to eventually move more students out of hotels and apartments and create a more permanent solution to help address increasing demand for on-campus housing,” according to the project summary.

That presentation noted a 38% increase in fall headcount, to 865 students, between 2021 and 2025.

USF Sarasota-Manatee still has priority over the campus facilities while it is teaching out students accepted prior to July. New College must also honor the original dorm rental agreements for USF students through next August, according to a USF timeline of the campus transfer.

DeSantis has made New College a priority in his efforts to transform higher education in the state. In recent years, the Republican governor has revamped the institution’s board and installed a political ally as president, making him one of the state’s highest paid college leaders. The college then set about dismantling its diversity, equity and inclusion initiatives as well as its gender studies department, and made other changes favored by conservatives.

#7081. U of Kentucky grants employee religious exemption from AI use

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A spokesperson said the university found the humanist administrator could still perform the essential functions of his job with the requested accommodation.

AI’s adoption has raised a number of compliance considerations for employers, and employment law experts have warned that religious objections to AI use could increasingly come to the forefront. That’s in part because religious discrimination claims have already climbed in the years following the COVID-19 pandemic, but it is also due to the ethical and moral questions that surround AI use.

In his statement, for instance, the University of Kentucky employee cited AI’s environmental costs, its “interference with people’s ability to earn a living” and its “supplanting and theft of human creativity.” The employee said these factors meant that use of AI would go against his conscience.

Jay Blanton, spokesperson for the university, said in an email that the institution reviews religious accommodation requests on an individual basis. It concluded that the employee in question, a research administrator, could perform the essential functions of his job with the requested accommodation.

“As technology and job responsibilities evolve, accommodations may be reviewed to ensure they remain reasonable and workable,” Blanton noted.

It’s not the first reported instance of a U.S. employee being granted religious accommodation with respect to AI. In June, Business Insider reported that a North Carolina-based Unitarian Universalist employee who worked as a software engineer received an AI accommodation after she raised environmental and ethical objections to the tech.

Under employment laws like Title VII of the 1964 Civil Rights Act, employers may deny an employee’s proposed religious accommodation if they can show that it poses an undue hardship.

But that calculation has shifted in recent years thanks to a landmark 2023 U.S. Supreme Court decision. In Groff v. DeJoy, SCOTUS held that, in order for a proposed accommodation to pose undue hardship, the burden must be substantial in the overall context of the employer’s business. Legal experts previously told HR Dive that this is a higher bar than the court’s prior precedent, which defined undue hardship as that which poses a “more than de minimis cost.”

#7080. Senior Product Designer, Developer Platform at LiveKit

Most product designers want to design for everyone. This role is for someone who'd rather design for one very specific, very opinionated person: the developer at 2am trying to figure out why their voice agent just failed a call. You'll design the tools developers use to test, evaluate and debug AI agents — a category that's still being invented, with no established patterns to copy. If dense data, real-time systems and reading a stack trace sound more fun than intimidating, keep reading.

You'll thrive as a Senior Product Designer if you:

are obsessed with making the best product for our customers

are known as the go-to person for tackling tough problems

are focused on polish, detail and quality

are a fast learner, frequently picking up new tips, tricks, and skills

The best way to impress us is with thoughtful ways you'd implement LiveKit, and potentially tinkering with it 😊

Own the design of how developers test, evaluate and observe their agents — simulations, evals, logs and agent insights

Turn complex, real-time data into interfaces that are easy to scan, understand and act on

Design new interaction patterns for AI-native workflows, where agents generate the first draft and humans adjust it

Prototype in code and work hand-in-hand with engineers to ship polished work

Contribute patterns to our design system for dense, debugging-oriented surfaces

You've shipped design work for developer tools, observability or data-heavy products

You have strong data-visualization instincts and know when a chart beats a table (and when it doesn't)

You're comfortable in code — reading PRs, tweaking Tailwind, building prototypes

You earn the trust of deeply technical users and engineers

Experience designing for AI or agent tooling

AI tools are already a core part of how you design

An opportunity to build something truly impactful to the world

Contribute to open source alongside world-class engineers

#7071. Want to improve the preschool-to-kindergarten handoff? Strengthen local connections

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An early childhood expert says early education “zones” and “hubs” can connect fragmented communities, benefiting schools, children and families.

School districts often treat kindergarten as the education starting point. But they can better support young students — and strengthen family relationships — by building connections with early childhood programs and community partners before students reach kindergarten, according to David Jacobson, director of First 10, an Education Development Center initiative supportive of early childhood school-community partnerships.

Children, their families and teachers benefit when their experiences connect across settings and years. For example, pre-K and kindergarten teachers might share information about instructional practices, understand each other’s standards, and build on students' familiar routines and learning styles so there is some continuity as the students transition from pre-K, Jacobson said.

This proactive relationship building, he said, can also benefit school systems that are eager to enroll and retain more students but face declining enrollments due to falling birthrates, school choice competition, changes in immigration policies and other factors.

"Instead of waiting for children to arrive in kindergarten and then trying to persuade families to stay, districts can work with early childhood providers, libraries and family-serving organizations to build trust, to support transitions, to align practice and address barriers to attendance," Jacobson said.

Jacobson, in a paper published this fall through the Stanford Center on Early Childhood, said that a child's years from birth through elementary school "represent a uniquely important developmental window." However, early childhood programs, elementary schools, health providers and family-serving agencies often operate in isolation of each other, he said.

To build better alignments, Jacobson suggests school-early education "zones" and "hubs" that have a shared geographical area and shared responsibility for children and families in their areas.

A school-early childhood zone consists of multiple elementary schools, early childhood programs and community partners within a defined geographical area. A hub includes similar work around just one elementary school. Individual schools can be part of both a zone and hub.

High-quality programs alone “don't fully take advantage of the opportunity we have to support children and families, and so all of those organizations can be much more effective working together than working individually,” Jacobson said.

He acknowledges that this work can be challenging, especially as school systems and early childhood education programs strive to improve their own practices and outcomes. But, Jacobson explained, a zone or a hub can be a "feasible, practical, realistic structure that encourage school districts to shift their mindsets to see younger children as soon-to-be students, and to see early childhood programs as essential partners."

Building alignment between early education programs and elementary schools is the "final vertical leap" in education, he said.

Other levels of education, for instance, have vertical approaches: When a district notices its 9th graders are struggling, it may examine the 8th grade experience. And community colleges often adjust their course offerings to align with career pathways offered at feeder high schools.

But principals and district leaders may be less familiar with the broad array and operation of early education programs, including Head Start, community-based programs and family-run centers, according to the Stanford paper.

"Districts need to adopt a broader set of strategies to engage and support families early, and they need coordinated early childhood partnerships," Jacobson said. "That's what zones and hubs are: coordinated early childhood partnerships that help them build these relationships and help them build familiarity, trust and continuity."

Several communities across the country have already implemented zones and hubs with the help of First 10, which has supported 50 communities across six states with aligning school-early education approaches.

First 10, for instance, worked to develop and strengthen a zone for Maine's Sheepscot Valley Regional School Unit 12, a rural district that includes seven towns and five elementary schools. The work revealed gaps between pre-K and kindergarten instructional alignment and in communication about rising kindergarten students. As a result, the First 10 team expanded the use of transition meetings from serving only students with disabilities to serving all the pre-K children advancing to kindergarten.

In addition to those transition conversations, pre-K and kindergarten teachers visited each others' classrooms, "leading to a broader effort to incorporate Pre-K more holistically into the leadership structures and general systems of the elementary schools," the Stanford paper said.

In the hub community of Russellville, Alabama, one veteran pre-K teacher had never visited a kindergarten classroom and never had a kindergarten teacher visit her classroom. But in 2022, the pre-K and kindergarten teachers began annual reciprocal visits, which led to ongoing joint professional learning for community-based preschool, Head Start, district pre-K and kindergarten educators. "For the first time, teachers across settings analyzed standards together, identified teaching challenges, and aligned strategies across grade levels," according to the Stanford paper.

First 10 zones and hubs often orient their work by introducing The Basics to early childhood educators and early grade teachers. The Basics is a public health approach organized around five evidence-based parenting principles:

In the zone community of Woonsocket, Rhode Island, the First 10 team and the city’s primary pediatric practice partnered to share information about The Basics with parents during children's wellness visits and also included the messages into “Welcome to Kindergarten” events.

Some First 10 communities celebrate the pre-K to kindergarten transition with a series of activities that bridge pre-K and kindergarten rather than just one event. In Pennsylvania's Conestoga Valley School District, the zone community created a “Kindergarten Express,” a coordinated train-themed series of transition “stops” with a kick-off, showcase, screening, summer meet-and-greet, camp and orientation to ease student and parent anxiety about the transition.

"We want safe, stable, and predictable experiences across the day, across years and across settings," Jacobson said. "So what children experience in Head Start or pre-K should connect with what they experience in kindergarten. What they learn in one year should prepare them for and be built upon in the next year. And when experiences like this are connected, children are more comfortable, learning is reinforced, and families experience a system that makes sense.”

Correction: An earlier version of this story misspelled the photographer’s name in the photo credits. The credits have been updated.
