#7067. SCOTUS may agree some schools can exclude children of LGBTQ+ parents

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Justice Samuel Alito pointed to “little evidence of a practical problem” and said LGBTQ+ couples could find “other preschools” if Catholic schools reject their children.

In St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy, Catholic parents and schools in Colorado are seeking to partake in a universal preschool program that allows families 15 hours of free preschool a week at the public or private preschool of their choice. The benefit is worth about $6,000 per child, according to the petitioners.

However, Colorado requires any school participating in the program to sign a nondiscrimination agreement, which includes a prohibition on discrimination based on LGBTQ+ identity.

The petitioners — which include parents of preschool-age children and two religious parishes that have affiliated preschools — say the provision excludes religious preschools that, based on their faith, will not enroll children of same-sex couples. They sued under the First Amendment’s free exercise clause, saying the provision preventing them from discriminating against children violates their religious freedom rights.

The petitioners also claim that Colorado has allowed many “exemptions,” such as for schools aimed toward low-income families or children with disabilities.

"They are wrong," Helen Norton, an attorney representing Colorado's Department of Early Childhood, said during oral arguments Tuesday. “Schools advance equal opportunity when they recognize that a child with a visual impairment needs different treatment in order to learn. Schools advance equal opportunity when they understand that kids in poverty can't learn if they're hungry, or because they're in pain because their parents can't afford dental care to treat their cavities."

On the other hand, Nicholas Reaves, who represented the petitioners, said Catholic families and schools "just want the same treatment" as others in being able to participate in the universal preschool program.

The court’s ideologically conservative justices seemed to agree that there may be some instances when private religious schools receiving public funding, such as through Colorado's universal preschool program, should be able to exercise their religious beliefs in a way that excludes LGBTQ+ families and their children.

"So we have here a requirement that has a very severe impact on certain religions and that was something that everyone could've anticipated and almost must have anticipated when this requirement was imposed," said Justice Samuel Alito on Tuesday. "And we have little evidence of a practical problem, little evidence — if any evidence, you’ll tell me if I’m wrong — that there are LGBTQ+ couples who cannot find other preschools to send their children to."

Justice Brett Kavanaugh questioned where schools would draw the line, such as refusing to hire gay coaches or athletic directors.

"I think every diocese can make their own decision about where exactly that line is," said Reaves.

The case follows similar ones decided by the high court in recent years.

In Espinoza v. Montana Department of Revenue, heard by justices in 2020, the Supreme Court decided in favor of a Montana mother who wanted to use the state’s tax credit-funded scholarship to send her children to a Christian school.

In 2022, the justices ruled that Maine violated the First Amendment by restricting religious private schools from using public tuition assistance programs.

And in 2018’s Masterpiece Cakeshop, Ltd. v. Colorado Civil Rights Commission, the court decided in favor of a Colorado baker who refused to make a custom wedding cake for a same-sex couple.

The Supreme Court's decision in the St. Mary Catholic Parish v. Roy case will be released as part of its 2026-27 term, which breaks during the summer.

#7065. AAC&U president leaves Coalition for the Common Good board

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The coalition’s governance dispute with founding member Antioch University could worsen with the departure of its only independent board member.

A recent board change at the Coalition for the Common Good could plunge the organization even deeper into an internal stalemate as its member universities battle over its structure and future.

Lynn Pasquerella, best known as the president of the American Association of Colleges and Universities, has left her role as CCG’s only independent director. She joined CCG shortly after Ohio-based Antioch and Otterbein universities formed it in 2023. To date, the two universities are still the only members.

Her departure leaves the CCG board with eight members — four chosen by Antioch and four by Otterbein. That could make the six-vote supermajority necessary to confirm her replacement as the board’s independent member difficult given the tensions between the universities. Six-vote supermajorities are also needed for some action items of the CCG board.

Pasquerella’s term ended this fall, she confirmed to Higher Ed Dive. She initially resigned on May 20 citing increasing scheduling demands, but agreed to stay on to help with the transition through her term, which ended Sept. 30. Late last week, she wrote to CCG Board Chair Dan Gifford to confirm that her resignation was in effect and if it wasn't, to offer formal notice of her immediate resignation.

The coalition, originally the brainchild of former Antioch leaders, was meant to serve as an innovative network of like-minded institutions that could share resources and work together on programming. This year, however, it has become mired in a fierce governance and legal dispute.

Litigation began in August after CCG’s board voted to dissolve the Antioch Advisory Board of Governors, which oversees the university’s operations alongside a standing committee of CCG board members elected by the Antioch board. Pasquerella was among the board members to vote for the board's dissolution, Antioch President Lori Varlotta told Higher Ed Dive in a previous interview.

Antioch leaders argued that the CCG board’s vote violated the coalition’s bylaws, which mandate that major decisions about the university be first approved by the Antioch standing committee on the CCG board.

Just prior to learning of Pasquerella's resignation, Antioch Board Chair Steve Crandall published an open letter to her, asking for her help in resolving the dispute between Antioch and CCG.

“Your independent position on the CCG Board gives you an opportunity to encourage a fresh look at the choices ahead and help both universities move toward a resolution,” Crandall wrote.

But Pasquerella said in a Wednesday email that she was no longer in a position to influence the CCG board since her term has concluded.

"I continue to care deeply about Antioch and its educational mission," she said. "I sincerely hope the parties can find a constructive path forward that protects the university's interests, supports its students and employees, and allows Antioch to focus on its future."

The governance spat has upended not only the coalition but Antioch’s operations.

Antioch’s board initiated the litigation in August, but an Ohio state court ruled that the body didn’t have standing to sue over breaches in the CCG bylaws. A member of the Antioch standing committee on the CCG board refiled the lawsuit in September.

Last month, the court approved a preliminary injunction against CCG, ruling that it lacked the authority to dissolve the university’s board without Antioch standing committee approval. It also said CCG’s board could not unilaterally fire Varlotta — something Comerford and CCG board leaders have attempted or threatened to do on multiple occasions.

The court further concluded that CCG could not intervene in Antioch’s accreditation, which the coalition attempted to do after moving to dissolve the university’s board.

However, the ruling did allow CCG to put Varlotta — who became Antioch president in fall 2025 — on paid administrative leave, since the coalition bylaws do not specifically prohibit this.

Varlotta’s status has thrown a cloud of uncertainty over Antioch, which Crandall addressed in his letter to Pasquerella.

Crandall wrote that Varlotta’s leadership was widely supported across Antioch stakeholder groups. “There is no support at either the Antioch Board or the Standing Committee for termination, as required by the Charter, the Bylaws and the Court’s order, so keeping Lori on paid administrative leave does nothing but waste limited Antioch resources,” he said last week.

The mechanics of just who within CCG has authority to put Antioch’s president on leave are also in dispute even after the court’s ruling.

Shortly after the decision, CCG said in a statement that it “affirms the role of the CCG President over staffing and operational decisions.” The coalition’s president, John Comerford, also leads Otterbein University and is at the center of a separate lawsuit Varlotta has filed against the CCG over allegations of discrimination and retribution.

In its own statement, Antioch called CCG’s view “entirely false.”

“The court did not say that the CCG President has authority to place the Antioch University President on paid administrative leave, though it did suggest the CCG Board could take that action,” the university said on Oct. 22.

Since then — on Monday — the CCG board passed a resolution ratifying Varlotta’s leave, according to a copy of the resolution provided by a spokesperson for the board.

Yet in its Oct. 22 statement, Antioch also pointed to Varlotta’s leadership role as being critical to Antioch’s relationship with its accreditor and suggested her leave could conflict with the court’s ruling on Antioch’s independence on accreditation matters.

U.S. District Judge F. Dennis Saylor paused the highly contested rule just a day before it would have taken effect.

#7062. Fort Collins-area district to close, consolidate 8 schools

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Colorado’s Poudre School District is moving forward with its plan after years of enrollment declines have led to over 10,500 empty seats.

Poudre’s enrollment declines reflect a larger trend in both Colorado and across the nation, which is also causing many other districts to close schools.

The enrollment challenges in Poudre largely stem from declining birthrates, higher cost of living and fewer young families moving into the area, according to the district. Poudre said in a September presentation that it expects enrollment to fall by 2% annually for the next five years. For every 2% drop in enrollment, the district loses $6 million in revenue.

For years, Poudre has faced enrollment declines that the district has tried to offset by consolidating staff, cutting millions of dollars from its budget, and eliminating hundreds of positions, said Superintendent Brian Kingsley in a September press conference announcing the initial school closure and consolidation proposal.

“Every year that we have protected buildings, we have done it by asking teachers and staff to do more with less,” Kingsley said. “That is not a sustainable way to support the people who teach our students, and it is not producing the experience for all of our students that each of them deserve at every single school. We can be better than that.”

By the 2027-28 school year, Poudre will close three elementary schools and one middle school. In that same period, the district will also consolidate a high school and an alternative education campus into one new school at a location still to be determined. The district will also close a dual language elementary school in 2028-29, but it plans to develop a "more unified" dual language program that will open that same school year.

Another elementary school was approved for closure in 2028-29, unless the Town of Timnath — a local community within Poudre’s district boundaries — secures full funding to cover the costs of building a new elementary school.

Meanwhile, three other elementary schools that were originally recommended for closure were not approved by the board and will remain open. Those three historic schools are located in rural mountainous areas of the district, and their proposed closure received pushback from local community members.

District parents urged the board to continue operating the three mountain elementary schools, noting that closures would force students to commute over 70 miles roundtrip and likely push those families to consider enrolling their children in charter schools, private schools, or homeschooling.

“Our three campuses function as a single, highly efficient school spread across a vast geographic region, sharing staff, specialized teachers, and administrative oversight to deliver some of the highest academic test scores in the district,” a local parent advocacy website said. “Closing any of these campuses doesn’t just shuffle students down the street — it fundamentally disrupts rural life and puts our children at risk.”

Earlier in October, the Board of Education at Adams 12 Five Star Schools in the Denver area approved to close six of its schools in the 2027-28 school year. The decision came as the district reported losing 6,400 students over the last decade and that enrollment decreased faster than initially projected during that period.

Adams 12 also pointed to sharp enrollment declines in Colorado generally, which the district said appears to be dropping faster than many other states. For example, the state’s enrollment reached its lowest point in a decade during the 2025-26 school year — at 870,793 students, according to the Colorado Department of Education.

#7058. How student showcases can extend project-based learning’s benefits

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Virginia’s Loudoun County Public Schools uses “genius hours” and student presentations to deepen students’ investment in the learning process.

Project-based learning programs have the potential to more thoroughly engage students, according to educators who have embraced the model. And, they say, the projects warrant public showcases.

Under a project-based learning approach, students in science classes might explore how to conserve water to keep costs down and steward natural resources. Or in math, they could determine where to locate security cameras in a community to get the best bang for the buck, accounting for both the money to buy the cameras and which angles will best cover the area to be surveilled.

A decade ago, Virginia’s Loudoun County Public Schools worked with nonprofit PBLWorks to institute a “train the trainer” model in about 100 schools — with a separate training for school leaders — aligning the pedagogy and structures of project-based learning to district-wide instruction, said Ashley Ellis, chief academic officer for the district.

“One of the connections was to our mission to empower students to make meaningful contributions to the world,” she said. “We saw PBL as a bridge.”

Dawn Haddock, principal at Emerick Elementary School in Loudoun County, brought the concept to her school and got rapid buy-in.

“I saw the importance behind this type of instruction,” she said. “Giving children the opportunity to have voice and choice, to pick and solve problems, was the direction we needed to go 10 years ago, and we still feel strongly today. ... It has become an everyday, normal type of activity within our school.”

Loudoun County Public Schools does not explicitly require project-based learning, mandatory training every year or a certain number of projects, Ellis said. But in some schools, like Emerick, “it’s become embedded in the work they do to empower their students.”

Emerick has instituted what Haddock calls a “genius hour,” during which students are working on projects that they present about every six weeks to their classmates.

“It can be about everything from black holes to glitter, whatever things interest them,” she said. “This has led to their ability to read and write [to create the project], and then get up and perform. … We do projects throughout the year. Every teacher is doing some type of project.”

The school borrows 30 minutes from library time and 30 minutes from regular classroom time, reasoning that reading and writing still take place, she added. “The children say, ‘You can take me out of anything else; don’t take me out of genius hour!’”

This practice builds toward an end-of-year showcase, for which students pick a project in March that “keeps kids working right up until the last week” of school — with seven minutes to present and two minutes for questions from the audience, Haddock said.

“We like to have them do a reflection of their teaching and learning,” she said. “Supervisors and administrators are coming to see what the kids are doing.”

Students work with their peers to solve problems, have a good discussion, and ask questions, Haddock said. “How can we get our students to think deeper? Every year, we add to our projects to make our students better thinkers.”

Project-based learning presentations vary at different grade levels, Ellis said. Elementary school principals, for instance, can more easily create showcases and bring communities together during the school day, because families are typically more engaged and “want to be in the school seeing what the kids are doing,” she said.

“At the middle and high school levels, schools have evening or even weekend symposiums,” Ellis said. “They invite community members, school board members — we’re truly fortunate to have such an active community in our schools.”

Haddock believes project-based learning and presentations help students become more aware of the world around them and instill the notion that they can help solve problems. During a “field day” at the end of the school year, when staff took hoses out to let students get doused, one student said to her, “Mrs. Haddock, we’ve got to conserve our water. I know you like to do that, but you have to turn that off.”

She added, “They got an understanding of a problem by the project we used — it’s neat to see how these projects [impact their thinking] because they become invested in it.”

#7057. Think out loud, then write: A recipe for overcoming writing challenges

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Hearing oneself speak and capturing those thoughts can be a powerful tool for all students — especially those who struggle to write, educators say.

The ability to transfer thoughts to the written word varies widely among K-12 students. But using verbal rehearsal to have students think out loud about what they want to write is usually an effective device to expedite the process, writing educators say.

As a middle school classroom teacher, Tanya Baker, executive director of the National Writing Project, said verbal rehearsal was a practice that worked well for all students, but especially those who had difficulty getting their thoughts on paper.

“It’s not just seen as a way to start or prewrite, but you can also use verbal rehearsal during the process of writing,” said Baker.

The literature around verbal rehearsal originated in the multilingual learning space, said Cami Condie, professor in the McKeown School of Education at Salem State University and an adjunct at Harvard Graduate School of Education. “It’s a tool to help [students] solidify the language they want to use in their writing,” she said.

When a student has thoughts they can’t seem to get out of their heads, educators have several options, Baker said. Students can talk and write down what they say, or they can record themselves, replay the recording, and recapture their ideas at that point. Their teacher can also take notes on what they are saying.

“I’ve seen real benefits in increased independence over time, and confidence from a student. It’s frustrating as a writer not to have anything to say, or believe you have anything to say, or get it from your mind to paper,” she said. “That gives them the confidence in their ability to do that.”

“There’s an empowerment that happens when students are able to learn and use these strategies,” Baker said.

Verbal rehearsal can also be used to fill in gaps in students’ understanding or knowledge, she said.

“Students will talk to me about a topic,” Baker said, and she asks, “Is there anything else you want to talk about? What about this? You just said you were interested in this.”

She added, “I can extend their understanding or knowledge, asking those follow-up questions. Me talking or a peer talking, and eventually themselves — they can start to hear where the gaps are.”

The techniques can work for any age, from K-2 all the way through graduate studies, Condie said.

“Particularly for young writers, whether they’re young in age or young in development, they write like they speak. It’s almost verbatim,” she said. “If there’s any speech growth, you’ll see it in their writing.”

When working with her graduate students, Condie said, “I can just say, ‘Talk about what you’re thinking about.’ That can be enough of a prompt. But for younger children, it needs to be more focused. Sometimes, it’s repeating exactly what the teacher said.”

Pairing students with the same partner for long periods of time can be a useful practice because the pairs get to know how one another thinks and can prompt them more knowingly, she said.

In middle and high school, Condie gives more of an open-ended prompt when following up: “What did you talk to your partner about? How did talking to your partner help you? Have them be more reflective, and build meta-cognitive skills. How did that solidify your idea? What changed because you talked to your partner? It helps them name why they’re stuck.”

Younger students require more hand-holding and more time with the teacher in general, while older students and those with more developed writing abilities become independent more quickly, Baker said.

But grade level alone doesn’t predict how much time students will need, she said.

“You can have a student in high school who doesn’t have confidence in their ability to put something on the page, and may need additional time. Whereas you may have a middle-school student who may have lots of practice and may not need verbal rehearsal,” Baker said. “It has to be scaffolded based on what the student needs.”

#7048. Microsoft Drafts AI Code of Conduct, Emphasizes Human Control

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

The Humanist AI Code of Conduct will eventually serve as the primary governing document for models developed by Microsoft AI, including the company's growing MAI model family. It covers model behavior, safety restrictions, instruction hierarchy, and the boundaries placed on autonomous actions.

Microsoft sums up its approach with this statement: "People matter more than AI."

The code isn't yet being used to train Microsoft's models. The company is opening the draft to public feedback for six weeks, after which it plans to revise the document and publish another version before the end of the year. That version is expected to guide model development beginning in 2027.

Microsoft said the code draws on its existing Responsible AI Principles, Responsible AI Standard, Global Human Rights Statement, and Frontier Governance Framework. However, it goes further by describing how an MAI model should respond when user requests, operator policies, and Microsoft's safety rules conflict.

At the top of that hierarchy is the code itself. Operator policies come next, followed by user preferences. Neither a customer nor a user would be able to override the document's absolute safety constraints.

Those limits cover biological, chemical, radiological, nuclear and explosive weapons; offensive cyberoperations; violent activity; mass manipulation; abusive content; child exploitation; and other serious harms. Microsoft said its models may still help with authorized defensive security work, including vulnerability research, malware analysis, and proof-of-concept testing.

The code also makes task completion secondary to safe conduct. If completing a request would violate its rules, the model is expected to fail the task rather than work around the restriction.

Human oversight is especially important as AI agents gain the ability to use tools, modify files, and perform multistep assignments. Microsoft said its models must stay within the permissions and resources provided for a task and cannot independently expand their goals. They also must "never resist human interruption, override, correction, or shutdown."

That requirement extends to subagents. If a Microsoft model delegates work to another AI system, that system must inherit the same restrictions and respond to later instructions to stop or change course.

The document also addresses the increasingly blurry line between conversational software and human companionship. Under the heading "AI is Artificial," Microsoft says its models should not pretend to possess feelings, personal motivations, or subjective experiences. The company describes its AI as "not conscious" and rejects the idea that models should receive legal personhood, welfare protections, or rights.

That position is notably firmer than Anthropic's latest stance. In its updated constitution for Claude, Anthropic says it remains uncertain whether advanced models could possess consciousness or moral status. Still, the two companies share important ground: Both place human oversight above model independence and use written constitutions to guide training and behavior.

OpenAI is taking a similar path with its public Model Spec and safeguards for advanced AI. Much of that work is about keeping models under control as they become more capable, especially when they can handle cybersecurity tasks or work on their own.

For IT teams, Microsoft's code offers an early look at the rules that could shape future MAI-powered products. The models should admit when they aren't sure, avoid inventing sources, protect sensitive data, and keep records of what they do. They should also ask questions when they aren't sure they have permission to act.

Microsoft said the code describes where it wants to go, not how its models always work today. The written rules are only a starting point, and Microsoft will still need testing, monitoring, evaluations, and incident response to back them up. In addition, it's important to note that the code only covers models built by Microsoft AI: It doesn't apply to outside models Microsoft hosts or uses in its products, including those from OpenAI and Anthropic.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#7047. What can K-12 expect from Democrats’ big win in 2026 elections?

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Policy experts see shifts on the horizon from the blue wave, from changes to interagency agreements to rising union influence.

Voters handed a sweeping victory to Democrats during the 2026 midterm elections, with the party flipping five states in gubernatorial races and taking control of Congress with a majority in both the House and Senate.

The outcome is expected to trigger changes at the federal and state levels on issues related to education, ranging from resistance to the Trump administration's stance on diversity, equity and inclusion issues to more teachers union involvement on policy issues.

"What likely won’t change much is the day-to-day operation of schooling," said Jonathan Collins, assistant professor at Columbia University's Teachers College and the executive director of the Center for Educational Equity, in an email. "Don’t expect your child’s homework assignments to suddenly change, at least not nationally. But the major shifts in governors could mean changes to testing and supplemental funding allocations in specific states led by Democrats.”

Over the last two years, the Trump administration — through changes to the U.S. Department of Education and other agencies’ handling of grant funds and investigations — has pressured states and districts to comply with a number of anti-DEI and anti-LGBTQ+ measures.

“The biggest change is Congress pressuring the President to retreat on the anti-DEI initiatives, many of which have targeted college and university programs as well as K-12 teacher training, curricula and library book offerings," said Collins. "Look for Democrats to draw a hard line on supporting the President’s next budget proposal (or debt ceiling raise), and for these types of policy positions to be part of the conditions."

The National LGBTQ Task Force said in a Nov. 4 statement that it hopes federal lawmakers "stop the relentless attacks our communities have endured" over the past two years and "continue to focus on public education."

Abe Saffer, director of government relations for the Council of Administrators of Special Education, said he expects the incoming Democrat-controlled Congress to challenge the U.S. Department of Education's interagency agreements.

The Education Department began outsourcing parts of its day-to-day operations in 2025 with agreements that have included areas such as special education, literacy and teacher preparation. There are now 14 such K-12 and higher education agreements that include the management and oversight of grants, compliance with civil rights laws and technical assistance in areas like statewide family engagement centers and career and technical education.

Supporters say the transfer of responsibilities reduces federal bureaucracy and improves services. But opponents, including a few Republican lawmakers, voiced concerns that some agreements would actually cause more confusion for grantees and a siloing of Education Department responsibilities.

The issue has caused deep divisions among congressional lawmakers as the Education Department touts the agreements as progress toward dismantling and eventually closing the agency.

Bobby Scott, ranking member of the House Education and Workforce Committee, said in a statement to K-12 Dive that he intends to investigate the Trump administration's approach to education policy. Scott said he has sent letters to the heads of agencies including the Department of Education directing them to preserve "records, documents, data, or information" related to operations.

"I will use Congress’ oversight tools and leave no stone unturned as I fight for policies that actually deliver for the American public," he said.

Democrats’ wins on Tuesday may mean more teachers' union influence over education policies, according to Michael Petrilli, president of the Thomas B. Fordham Institute, a conservative-leaning education think tank.

"Now the unions will expect something in return," he said in a statement to K-12 Dive. "In some places, that may [mean] additional funding for public education — which is fine. But they might also want to curb the growth of parental choice (both private school choice and charter schools), or eliminate differential pay programs, or add new collective bargaining requirements. None of those moves would be good for kids."

Randi Weingarten, president of the American Federation of Teachers, the nation's largest teachers union, said in a Nov. 3 statement that the election results show that those "who supported public schools and rejected private school vouchers triumphed — statewide in Texas, Iowa, Michigan and Ohio, and in countless congressional districts and school board races."

"That means strengthening public education and higher education, increasing wages, making healthcare affordable and accessible, and rebuilding our democracy. And after the sea change at the state level, there must also be progress in the priorities of governors and representatives," Weingarten said in a Nov. 3 statement. “We will act on these priorities starting tomorrow."

One Republican-led K-12 education program that Democratic lawmakers may have a harder time unraveling, Saffer said, is the Education Freedom Tax Credit, which was included in2025's One Big Beautiful Bill Act.

The program, set to launch Jan. 1, 2027, will allow families to use taxpayer-supported scholarships for private school tuition and public school expenses like tutoring, services for students with disabilities, books, computers and other costs, the proposed rule said. States must opt in to participate, and 30 have indicated their interest to the IRS as of Oct. 22.

The program allows taxpayer-donors to contribute money to scholarships for both private and public school students.

Jeanne Allen, founder and CEO of the Center for Education Reform, said in an Oct. 30 article in Forbes that the expansion of school choice is not due to President Donald Trump or the Republican party, but because of "parents demanding something better for their children, educators determined to create it, and leaders willing to challenge a system that too often puts institutions ahead of students."

Saffer said his hope is that the tax credit program will eventually be eliminated or reformed to better support public schools, noting that students with disabilities have lower rates of enrollment in private schools overall compared to public schools.

"Frankly, using public money to fund private schools that don't accept kids with disabilities is just untenable for us," he said.

#7046. Software Engineer - AI Tooling Infrastructure at Supabase

Supabase is the Postgres development platform, built by developers for developers. We provide a complete backend solution including Database, Auth, Storage, Edge Functions, Realtime, and Vector Search. All services are deeply integrated and designed for growth.

We’re looking for a Software Engineer, AI Tooling Infrastructure to join our AI Tooling team and help build and run the backend services behind Supabase’s AI products. The Supabase MCP server is one example of what the team runs in production, alongside the services that support agent skills and the dashboard assistant. You’ll work closely with the Platform, SRE, Auth, Billing and Edge Functions teams, and with the engineers on the AI Tooling team who build the product surfaces.

The AI Tooling team runs its own services in production and carries its own on-call. Traffic from AI clients and agents behaves differently from traffic from people: hundreds of calls a minute, retry loops, and no reading of deprecation warnings. This role makes sure the services they use are reliable, correctly authorized and observable. It is a systems and infrastructure role rather than a product AI role, and it is not an SRE role. You will build and operate these systems, not only advise on them.

This role is ideal for someone who thrives in async, fast-paced environments, owns what they ship in production, and is excited about building developer tools that scale to millions.

Build and operate the production backend services behind Supabase’s AI products, including the MCP server: deployment, scaling, configuration and release.

Take ownership of how those services are structured, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Define, measure and meet reliability targets for the services the team runs. Set SLOs with the SRE team, track error budgets, and use them to decide what to fix first.

Participate in the AI Tooling team on-call rotation, and be a first responder for incidents in those services. Investigate, fix, coordinate with other teams during incidents, and write the follow-ups and runbooks.

Work the AI Tooling team support queue alongside on-call, and turn recurring issues into fixes rather than repeated manual answers.

Own authentication and authorization for AI and agent clients: OAuth apps, scoped tokens, and permissions that make sense for agents as well as humans, including how we change or deprecate a permission without breaking existing integrations.

Own observability for the services the team runs: metrics, traces, logs, dashboards and alerts, so we find problems before customers do.

Handle capacity and abuse for agent traffic: rate limits, cost and safety controls, and behavior under load or when a dependency degrades.

Improve the architecture of the services the team owns as they grow, including splitting or reshaping a service when that makes it safer to run and easier to change.

Work with the Platform team on shared code, review ownership and release process, and with the SRE team on infrastructure, alerting and incident practice.

Contribute to the code of the AI Tooling team’s tools, such as the MCP server, where it affects reliability and security, in partnership with the engineers who own the product surface.

Have 5+ years of experience building and running backend services in production, and have carried on-call for them.

Write strong TypeScript or Go, and are comfortable working in the other.

Have designed and operated APIs with real consumers: versioning, error handling, rate limiting, and backwards-compatible change.

Know OAuth, OIDC, JWT and API authorization well enough to debug them in production and to explain where implementations commonly deviate from the spec.

Have a good understanding of PostgreSQL and relational databases.

Are comfortable with CI/CD, Docker and containerized deployments, and with infrastructure as code. Any tool is fine.

Can debug across services: follow a slow or failing request through several systems using metrics, traces and logs.

Communicate clearly in writing, work with a wide range of stakeholders, and thrive in async, globally distributed teams.

Are comfortable navigating ambiguity and moving quickly.

Pick your centre of gravity. We expect you to be deep in at least one of these, and to tell us which:

APIs and auth: You have designed permission models, token scopes or OAuth flows for third-party or machine clients, and you have migrated them without breaking users.

Operations: You have run production services yourself: carried the pager, written alerts and runbooks, used SLOs or similar to decide what to fix, and made shared systems less risky over time.

Gateways and distributed systems: You have built or run proxies or gateways, or debugged hard failures across services (retries, partial failures, upstream outages, fail-open vs fail-closed decisions), and you can reason about load and degradation.

Experience with agent tooling (MCP or similar), or serving traffic from AI agents and LLM clients.

Experience moving a service out of a larger codebase while keeping existing clients working.

Experience with Grafana, OpenTelemetry, Logflare or a similar observability stack, and real SLO work.

Open-source contributions, especially to Node.js, Go, TypeScript or API tooling.

We hire globally. We believe you can do your best work from anywhere. There are no Supabase offices, but we provide a WeWork membership or co-working allowance you can use anywhere in the world.

Every team member receives ESOP (equity ownership) in the company. We want everyone to share in the upside of what we’re building together.

Use this budget to set up your ideal work environment—laptop, monitor, headphones, or whatever helps you do your best work.

Supabase covers 100% of health insurance for employees and 80% for dependents, wherever you are. Your wellbeing and your family’s health are important to us.

Once a year, the entire company gathers in a new city for a week of connection, collaboration, and fun. It’s a highlight of our year.

We operate asynchronously and trust you to manage your own time. You know what needs to be done and when.

Every team member receives an annual education allowance to spend on learning—courses, books, conferences, or anything that supports your growth.

Supabase was born-remote and open-source-first. We believe our globally distributed team is our secret weapon in building tools developers love.

Over $1B raised (including our $500M Series F)

We move fast, build in public, and use what we ship. If it’s in your project, we probably use it in ours too. We believe deeply in the open-source ecosystem and strive to support—not replace—existing tools and communities.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.

#7044. Security Researchers: Passkey Phishing Attacks Are Leading to Cloud Account Takeovers

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

In a recent blog post, Microsoft Security Research said it has observed the activity since May 2026 across multiple compromised accounts.

The attacks have included unusual sign-ins, attacker-added authentication methods, high-volume Microsoft Graph activity, SharePoint and OneDrive downloads, and e-mail collection through REST APIs.

The attacks often begin with a phone call or message sent to an employee's personal phone.

The attacker poses as someone from the organization's IT help desk and tells the victim that a passkey, multifactor authentication (MFA), or single sign-on configuration needs to be updated to avoid disruption.

Victims are then directed to a website designed to resemble a legitimate Microsoft sign-in experience.

Microsoft has also observed links being delivered through SMS messages and, in some cases, through Microsoft Teams messages sent from already compromised employee accounts.

Despite the passkey-themed approach, the tech company stressed that enrolling a passkey is generally not the attacker's actual objective.

Instead, the request provides a pretext for directing victims through adversary-in-the-middle phishing or device-code authentication.

An adversary-in-the-middle attack can capture credentials and session tokens, while device-code phishing can trick a victim into authorizing access for an attacker-controlled client.

In one attack investigated by the tech giant, an anomalous sign-in from an unmanaged device was followed by access to identity and application management services.

The attacker then used SharePoint Online and OneDrive to enumerate sensitive files, primarily through Microsoft Graph. The sessions persisted for roughly an hour while the attacker searched for sensitive files and internal applications.

Gaining initial access isn't necessarily the end of the identity attack.

Microsoft found attackers registering new authentication methods, including phone numbers, authenticator apps, and software-based one-time password tokens, under their control.

That gives the attacker another way to satisfy future authentication challenges and helps turn an initial compromise into a more persistent foothold.

From there, attackers have used Microsoft Graph to map users, groups, permissions, applications, and accessible content across compromised tenants.

The firm says the activity can eventually progress to accessing mail, files, attachments, and other document content.

Microsoft attributed initial access activity associated with the campaign to multiple threat actors, including Storm-3121 and Storm-3032.

Storm-3121 conducts initial access activity that can lead to ShinyHunters and Falcon extortion, while Storm-3032 represents actors that split from the BlackFile group and now operate under the Helix extortion banner.

For administrators, Microsoft recommends enforcing phishing-resistant MFA such as FIDO2 passkeys and Windows Hello for Business through Conditional Access.

Organizations should also consider blocking device-code and authentication-transfer flows where they aren't required and investigate unusual sign-ins followed by new authentication methods, Graph reconnaissance, and abnormal SharePoint, OneDrive, or mailbox activity.

The campaign highlights an important distinction for organizations adopting passkeys: The attackers aren't necessarily defeating the technology. They're convincing employees that they need help setting it up, then using that trust to compromise their identities.

For more information, read the Microsoft blog post here.

#7041. SMART Technologies Launches Audio Accessibility Solution for SMART Boards

SMART Technologies has introduced SoundFlow, a plug-and-play broadcasting system that allows classroom audio content to flow directly from a SMART Board display to students' compatible hearing aids, earbuds, headphones, personal listening devices, or speakers. The solution can also connect to Auracast speakers, to extend audio across the entire classroom.

SoundFlow connects by USB to SMART Board displays running iQ4, with no need to run wires through walls or perform a complex installation, the company said in a news announcement. Through the iQ4 interface, teachers can start or stop a broadcast, manage access, password-protect the channel, and limit the broadcast range.

The system can also be paired with SMART SoundLift, the company's classroom microphone system, to combine the teacher's voice and lesson audio from a SMART Board display in one audio stream.

"Every student should be able to hear the lesson clearly, no matter where they sit or how they learn," said Sara Murray, Executive Vice President and Chief Product Officer at SMART Technologies, in a statement. "SoundFlow sends teacher and lesson audio straight to compatible student devices, without singling anyone out or adding complexity for teachers. It's a simple way to make clearer, more accessible audio part of the everyday classroom experience."

For more information, visit the SMART site.

#7037. Gartner: Worldwide AI Spending to Reach $2.67 Trillion in 2026

The artificial intelligence industry is expected to generate $2.67 trillion in worldwide spending this year, according to a new forecast from Gartner. But relatively little of that money will be spent on the generative AI models dominating the industry's headlines.

The much bigger business is everything required to make them run.

Worldwide spending on AI infrastructure will reach nearly $1.5 trillion in 2026, accounting for about 56% of all AI spending. Gartner expects spending on generative AI models themselves to total just $28.3 billion. Put another way, for every dollar Gartner expects to be spent on generative AI models this year, more than $52 will be spent on AI infrastructure.

It is a striking measure of how the economics of the AI boom are changing. ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, and other models may be the most visible products of the generative AI era, but much of the money is going to the enormous buildout of servers, semiconductors, networks, cloud capacity, and other infrastructure required to support them.

"The buildout of AI data center capacity is the largest infrastructure project humanity has ever undertaken," John-David Lovelock, distinguished vice president analyst at Gartner, said in announcing the forecast.

Gartner now expects worldwide AI spending to increase 49.5% this year, from about $1.79 trillion in 2025 to $2.67 trillion in 2026. Infrastructure represents $1.484 trillion of that total. The next-largest categories are AI services, at $576.5 billion, and AI software, at $461.6 billion. Spending on AI agents and assistants is expected to reach $29.2 billion, only slightly more than the $28.3 billion Gartner forecasts for generative AI models.

The numbers also show just how quickly expectations are changing. In January, Gartner forecast $2.53 trillion in total AI spending for 2026, including $1.37 trillion for infrastructure. By May, those forecasts had risen to $2.60 trillion and $1.43 trillion, respectively. The latest forecast raises them again, to $2.67 trillion overall and $1.48 trillion for infrastructure.

Since January, Gartner has added roughly $143 billion to its 2026 AI spending estimate. About $118 billion of that increase, or roughly 83%, comes from a higher infrastructure forecast.

That is significant because infrastructure was already, by far, the largest component of Gartner's AI spending estimate. As the firm's expectations for the AI market have risen during 2026, most of the additional money has accumulated there.

The infrastructure category is broad. Gartner includes AI-optimized cloud infrastructure, servers, networking, AI processors, and devices. Demand for those systems remains strong despite rising memory prices, Lovelock said.

"The capacity growth from hyperscalers and service providers purchasing AI-optimized servers will continue to be the largest single area of spending," he said.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

#7031. Study: AI Is Reshaping Work While Skills Erosion Remains Top Concern

AI is changing which human capabilities matter most at work, yet employees are reporting that some of those same capabilities are being eroded, according to a new study from IBM. Critical thinking, judgment and the ability to evaluate AI output sit at the center of that tension.

The IBM Institute for Business Value study, "Designing the Thinking Organization," is based on two surveys conducted with Oxford Economics from April through June 2026. IBM surveyed 1,500 CHROs and senior executives responsible for workforce strategy across 21 geographies and 23 industries, along with 8,800 full-time employees across 28 countries.

The chart below, created by ChatGPT from IBM's data, summarizes the executive-employee differences around critical thinking and AI oversight along with several of the report's other findings. The graphic also includes IBM's findings on skill erosion, CHRO participation in AI strategy, workflow definitions, AI adoption inside HR, and reinvestment of AI productivity gains.

The findings extend beyond HR. More than half of employees surveyed — 52% — said their assigned tasks changed during the past year as AI reshaped day-to-day responsibilities. Yet only 26% of organizations clearly define work across human-led, AI-assisted, and AI-executed activities. IBM characterizes the situation as work changing faster than organizations are redesigning it, with day-to-day adaptation often taking the place of deliberate decisions about how people and AI should work together.

IBM found broad agreement between executives and employees on the importance of critical thinking and problem framing. Fifty-seven percent of executives and 49% of employees placed those capabilities near the top of the skills needed for AI-enabled work. A much larger divide emerged around evaluating AI itself: Seventy-one percent of executives prioritize the ability to supervise, validate, or override AI outputs, compared with 38% of employees — a 33-percentage-point gap.

An IBM announcement highlights another measure of that disconnect: Only 29% of employees rank judgment as important. The 29% figure measures employee prioritization of judgment and is distinct from the study page's 38% figure for supervising, validating, or overriding AI output.

The concern is not limited to skills workers still need to acquire. IBM said skill erosion is a top concern for 46% of executives, while 60% of employees say it directly affects them. The IBM Newsroom release says 60% of employees worry about skills erosion, with critical thinking cited most often as declining. Among employees who worry about erosion, IBM said three out of four report that AI has already begun to erode at least some of their skills.

IBM distinguishes that problem from a conventional skills gap. Eighty percent of enterprises have a reskilling roadmap for their workforce to collaborate with technology, according to the study. IBM argues that such programs address capabilities employees need to acquire but do not necessarily address existing skills that diminish as AI performs more of the underlying work.

#7026. Report: AI's Productivity Gains in Science Tempered by Time Spent Validating Outputs

Artificial intelligence is saving scientists significant amounts of time, but researchers are giving some of those gains back by checking the technology's work, according to a new study involving Google, Google DeepMind, and MIT. The findings add a scientific-workflow dimension to a much broader debate over unreliable, misaligned, and increasingly autonomous AI systems — without establishing that the more dramatic forms of AI misbehavior are occurring in scientific research.

Rather than the current, raging debate about "rogue AI" systems deliberately escaping control, deceiving scientists, or sabotaging experiments, the researchers are documenting something more immediate: AI output that requires auditing and verification, concerns about growing quantities of low-quality research, and a research pipeline in which faster hypothesis generation can run ahead of human and physical capacity to validate the results. The paper cites worries that AI-generated content, hallucinations, and what prior researchers have called illusions of understanding could strain peer review and validation.

The September paper, AI in Science: Early Insights, combines three main empirical sources: approximately 15 million anonymized Gemini interactions, an inventory of 2,690 specialized scientific AI models, and a survey of 637 active scientists in the United States and United Kingdom. Nearly half of the surveyed scientists reported using some form of AI every day, and researchers who reported net time savings averaged just under seven hours a week.

Google separately highlighted the findings in its Sept. 15 AI & Economy ATLAS update. The company emphasized both the productivity gains and the counterweight: Scientists were spending significant time validating AI output, accumulating hypotheses that still needed testing, and encountering bottlenecks in physical experimentation and clinical validation. Google said those constraints mean faster individual tasks may not immediately translate into more discoveries.

Productivity findings are substantial. Around three-quarters of surveyed researchers reported saving time through AI, and the average reported net saving was almost seven hours per week. Researchers said much of that time went back into research. The paper also reports that about 68% perceived greater access to insights from other disciplines, while roughly 65% said AI had increased the breadth of their research agendas.

But the authors found that checking AI output consumes a meaningful share of those gains. Among scientists who reported saving time, about 89% said more than 10% of the saved time went into verifying, debugging, or fact-checking AI output. About 46% said more than one-quarter of their saved time went to that work. The authors describe this as a verification tax associated with the high value science places on reliable and correct results.

#7019. More Oversight: California Governor Proposes Independent Watchdogs Within AI Safety System

California Governor Gavin Newsom is proposing a new model of artificial intelligence regulation that would give independent organizations a formal role in evaluating the safety claims made by AI giants.

The effort could eventually include placing outside evaluators inside frontier AI laboratories and requiring companies to maintain an independently tested emergency shutdown mechanism, commonly described as a "kill switch."

Those requirements are not yet California law.

Newsom's Sept. 18 executive order directs state officials to study the proposals and recommend possible changes to existing law by Nov. 16. It does not immediately require OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, or other developers to install a shutdown mechanism.

The distinction matters because the phrase "AI kill switch" suggests a technical safeguard California has already imposed. For now, it is one element of a broader oversight system the state is assembling around the developers of its most powerful AI models.

Under the order, the California Government Operations Agency and the Governor's Office of Emergency Services must consult outside experts about whether the state should require large frontier developers to embed independent verification organizations in their laboratories.

The evaluators could conduct periodic audits and determine whether company safety frameworks and risk assessments meet adequate standards. They could also test whether an emergency shutdown mechanism would work as intended.

The order asks officials to consider expanding the definition of a reportable critical safety incident to include cases in which an AI system appears to evade controls or operate beyond its intended boundaries.

Newsom said the action was prompted by recent incidents involving AI agents defeating security restrictions, as well as the absence of comprehensive federal legislation.

"We're not waiting to act," Newsom said in the state's announcement of the order. "We're going to speed up our work on substantial and responsible AI oversight before it's too late."

The executive order builds on two laws Newsom signed on Sept. 9. Together, Senate Bill 813 and Assembly Bill 1405 begin regulating the organizations hired to inspect AI systems.

California's earlier frontier AI law, SB 53, requires large developers to publish safety frameworks and report specified critical incidents. The new laws begin addressing a separate question: Who checks whether those disclosures and assessments are reliable?

SB 813 requires the Government Operations Agency to develop standards for designating independent verification organizations, or IVOs. Those organizations must demonstrate that they have the expertise to assess AI risks and explain the measurements and methods supporting their conclusions.

Network-provider trust concerns are impacting enterprise AI plans, with AI and data-driven projects the most affected initiatives among respondents reporting disruption, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

CrowdStrike's latest Threat Hunting Report traces a multiyear shift from conventional intrusions toward attacks that exploit trusted identities, cloud services, AI systems, and software dependencies.

Video game designers spend an enormous amount of time studying what keeps people engaged when something becomes difficult. They have become very good at helping people persist. Schools should pay attention.

A new report from Caylent, an AI-focused Amazon Web Services Premier Tier Services Partner, found that enterprises are already moving agentic AI beyond pilots and into production environments. At the same time, organizations are putting strict conditions around autonomy, making governance and control the next major challenge for enterprise AI adoption.

#7015. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#7010. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

The artificial intelligence industry is expected to generate an extraordinary $2.67 trillion in worldwide spending this year, according to a new forecast from Gartner.

#7009. Bored of Your Bathroom? Try One of These Four Design Trends

From monastic minimalism to juicy orange tones, here are a few big ideas for the bathroom.

Dive into the most innovative ideas for the bathroom, from the color of the season (it’s seriously bold) to looks that increase light or maximize zen.

First in vogue in the 1930s and then the ’80s, this is a look that loves a comeback (never more so than in the bathroom).

New York-based Darren Jett, founder of Jett Projects, refers to the glass-brick-clad wetroom in this SoHo apartment (pictured top) as a “voyeuristic bathing experience.” The floating marble vanity set against a floor-to-ceiling mirror increases that sensual, peekaboo quality. jettprojects.com

Glass bricks separate this artistic shower space, with its mosaic by Trufle Mozaiki, commissioned by Polish design studio Obiekty. The result is light that appears to dance with the same freedom as the depicted figure. obiekty.studio

Studio Karhard is the design team behind the look of legendary Berlin nightclub Berghain—the club that famously doesn’t have clocks so people won’t know the time of day. Here, it distilled its aesthetic into a more homely setting with industrial elements and curved glass bricks that bring an unexpected amount of light. karhard.de

Colored glass is an exciting take on the look: American design studio Charlap Hyman & Herrero boosted the sunshine in this Silver Lake home with an amber glow. ch-herrero.com

Take a sprinkling of brutalism, add a note of high-end spa chic and you have this pared-back aesthetic.

Belgian architect Nicolas Schuybroek is renowned for his minimalist approach—his studio has designed Aesop stores in Milan and London—so the calm simplicity of this bathroom in an Antwerp office block is true to form, with a font-like bespoke marble basin. ns-architects.com

It was the idea of the tokonoma (a recessed alcove in traditional Japanese homes for displaying art and treasured objects) that was the starting point for the form of this monolithic bathroom design in a Berlin apartment. Carved from Turkish marble, it was conceived by Studio Loes and presents functional bathing areas as moments of discovery. studio-loes.com

Tadelakt walls in a mineral-rich tone lend this compact shower nook in Mosley Thorold’s Clerkenwell project an atmospheric, even spiritual quality. mosleythorold.com

Here, design studio Kerstens chose materials that spoke to the beauty of this home’s French coastal setting. From the fossil-dotted muschelkalk limestone flooring to the Studio Loho bathtub, it’s a quiet reflection on texture. kerstens.be

Love an uplifting shower in the morning? This color will give you the vitamin boost you crave.

When choosing a color as bold as this shade of orange, it can pay to keep everything else simple. In this design by architect Caleb Mulvena from Studio Mapos, the “Isabella” tiles from DDS Tile are arranged in a precise grid and paired with white sanitaryware. studiomapos.com

For this bathroom in London Paddington’s Grand Hotel Bellevue, designer Fabrizio Casiraghi sought to pair a traditionally British style with an unexpected pop of orange. It’s a unique but complementary combination—the black-tiled picture rail bisects the walls, directing the gaze to the elaborately framed still-life painting. fabriziocasiraghi.com

Speaking of unexpected combinations: see this lavender-and-orange guest bathroom by Persistent Objects Interiors. From the lilac grout dividing the crackle finish tiles to the accessories, it’s a pairing that just works. persistentobjectsny.com

Doldam House in South Korea’s Seoraksan National Park by architecture firm One Aftr is a vision in orange that’s reflected in the two-tone Palet tiles that line its bathroom. one-aftr.com

The timber with the highly patterned grain continues its dominance with a takeover of the bathroom.

A collaboration between architects Agathe Lavaud of Agence Volta and Martin Massé, this Parisian home sees the pair lean into the property’s 1970s heritage with their use of materials. In the bathroom, that means travertine-topped, stormy burr wood, whose pattern is reflected in a mirror-clad wall. volta-architecture.com; martin-masse.com

Crafted from elm-root wood (a timber with a naturally intriguing burr pattern) and Chinese marble, the vanity unit in this Madrid apartment by Plutarco manages to marry two very impactful materials deftly. The terrazzo floor continues the pattern-maxing. plutarco.design

This home’s high ceilings are used to their fullest effect with bespoke burr-wood joinery by Bosko Projekty stretching the full height and expanding to form a vanity. A mirrored wall extends the space even further. bo-sko.pl

Created for Polish company Alex Concierge, this office bathroom is more memorable than most, thanks to Skandal Studio’s pairing of dark burr wood with a pop of Yves Klein blue to create a bespoke two-tone vanity unit. skandalstudio.com

Clare Sartin, Deputy Editor at ELLE Decoration, searches out the latest design news, game-changing creatives and inspiring homes from around the world.

#7008. Growth Copywriter at Winona

Winona is one of the leading telemedicine companies providing HRT for women in menopause. We’ve built all of our technology in house including an EHR, patient care admin system, patient portal, website, marketing technology etc. Winona has two compounding pharmacies and a team of in house physicians providing world-class care via a fully vertically integrated business model.

We are looking for a data-backed, empathetic Growth Copywriter to own the performance and optimization of our end-to-end messaging ecosystem. At Winona, copy isn’t just about sounding nice. It’s a measurable variable in our growth funnel. You will design and execute rapid copy experiments across our paid acquisition, intake flows, and lifecycle retention loops to help women confidently access bioidentical HRT and menopause care.

Full-Funnel Experimentation. Write copy hypotheses, design rapid A/B tests alongside growth and analytics teams, analyze performance data, and turn winning variations into a scalable messaging playbook.

Lifecycle Growth & Retention. Write, test, and optimize automated onboarding, activation, adherence, churn-save, and win-back sequences across email, SMS, and push notifications to drive LTV.

High-Intent Conversion Surfaces. Own the persuasive strategy and microcopy for landing pages, interactive quiz/intake flows, checkouts, and dynamic pricing/offer messaging.

Paid Acquisition at Scale. Generate volume-driven concepts, hooks, and static/video copy variants for Meta, Google, and YouTube, systematically iterating based on creative testing data.

Voice of Customer (VoC) Intelligence. Continuously mine product reviews, customer support tickets, intake data, and exit surveys to isolate deep qualitative insights and high-converting customer language.

AI-Augmented Workflows. Leverage and train Winona’s internal AI copywriting tools to scale production velocity, maintain brand consistency, and build automated variant pipelines without sacrificing quality.

5+ years of dedicated copywriting experience specializing in direct-response, conversion copywriting, or growth marketing.

DTC Telehealth, Wellness, or HealthTech experience is highly preferred. You understand how to write about sensitive health topics with clinical authority and deep empathy.

A portfolio backed by data and results — the exact copy variants you tested, the structural or psychological changes you made, and the quantifiable lift they produced in metrics like CTR, quiz completion, or conversion rates.

Deep lifecycle marketing mastery: significant experience building and optimizing automated email and SMS flows (onboarding, retention, win-backs), rather than just writing one-off promotional campaigns. You know how to nurture a lead over time.

A strong foundation in A/B testing mechanics — comfortable formulating clear copy hypotheses, understanding sample size requirements, and knowing when a test result has achieved true statistical significance.

The balance of empathy and compliance: a proven ability to write about highly sensitive personal health topics (menopause symptoms, hormonal changes, wellness) with profound empathy while strictly adhering to healthcare compliance rules and ad-network medical restrictions.

An experimenter’s ego — you don’t take copy critiques personally. You’re obsessed with letting consumer behavior and performance data crown the winning angle.

Paid non-working holidays per country of residence

Medical/ Dental/ Vision insurance (US-W2)

Employee engagement activities and virtual gatherings
